# bill-text-s8 — §7, the open-question item list: items 1 to 134

**Moved out of [`bill-text-s8`](/MOO/issues/MOO-42#document-bill-text-s8) in store revision 59**, on the Policy Director's answer to §7 item 112 of 12 October 2026. Every numbered item, option, recommendation, table row and lettered label is preserved verbatim, so a reference of the form **§7 item n** resolves here. The bill document's §7 is now a pointer stub.

**Two things changed in the move and both are marked where they occur.** Seven items are dispositioned on the Director's direction of 12 October 2026 — items 88, 108, 109, 110, 111, 112 and 113 — and three are new: items 123, 124 and 125. Two stranded sentence ordinals are corrected. Nothing else is reworded.

**Items 114 to 121 were written after the §7.1 retained-record heading in store revision 57**, which put eight live items inside a stub that says its content was moved out, and left the list reading 1 to 113, then 122, then 114 to 121. The §7.1 heading and its three paragraphs now sit at the **end** of this document, after item 125, and **the numbered items run 1 to 125 in order**. Only positions changed: the reassembled file is **character-for-character the same length** as the file before the reordering, which is how the move was verified.

**INTERNAL WORKING DRAFT. Not a Division of Legislative Services bill, not reviewed or cleared by counsel, not legally sufficient for introduction. Pre-decisional and internal. Nothing here is cleared for external use.**

---

## 7. Open questions for Policy Director

**What MOO-46 closed.** Three of revision 2's questions are answered and are off this list: the
acceleration counterfactual (item 3 of MOO-46 directed the repair and revision 2 had already drafted
it; **[MOO-53](/MOO/issues/MOO-53) has now confirmed it against a run** — the structure is right, the
double count is gone, the standard forecloses both zero-acceleration readings with margin, and the two
indeterminacies it found are drafted out in revision 5, §6.2); the per-member ceiling's *basis* (a
rule, not a figure — though the figure itself is now item 3 below); and the meaning of the
$3,000,000 (the appropriation, not a forecast, with the rate–cap linkage withdrawn). Revision 2's
item 7 — "the cap funds 56 percent of the design's own central-case demand" — is **not** closed by
that reframing but is changed by it: if $3,000,000 is the appropriation then exhaustion and the
denial rate are features to disclose rather than a mis-set dial, which is what item 9 below now asks.
**Revision 9: the FY2031 and 58 percent figures this paragraph carried are withdrawn** — superseded by
MOO-53 §6.2 and then by [MOO-48](/MOO/issues/MOO-48), and **no cap-utilisation figure is authorised
at all** while [MOO-71](/MOO/issues/MOO-71) is open. The question item 9 asks does not depend on the
figure, and it stays open.

**Two of these are new and both come out of MOO-46 item 4.** Items 1, 2 and 3 must be answered before
this goes to DLS or to a patron.

3. **The per-member ceiling figure — DRAFTED AT $170,000, AND REVISION 21 RE-OPENS ITS BASIS WITHOUT
   RE-OPENING THE FIGURE.** $170,000 was walked off the per-member certified-subsidy distribution at
   the **0-month** rung (90th percentile $160,468, maximum $208,905). The Director has now chosen the
   **12-month** rung, and a longer window moves every per-member subsidy down — later commencement,
   less acceleration charged — so the percentile the figure was chosen to sit at is not where
   $170,000 sits any more. **I have not moved the figure**: it is the Director's number, it is
   unbracketed in the Code text, and moving it on my own arithmetic would be exactly the error
   MOO-45 warned against. [MOO-85](/MOO/issues/MOO-85) asks Fiscal & Impact Modeler for the
   distribution at the 12-month rung and for the figure the Director's own basis returns there; if it
   is materially different the Director should see both numbers before this goes to a patron. **The
   record of the original choice follows.** The Director
   directed the figure on 11 October 2026 and D 2 now carries it unbracketed. **Three things about it
   that are still live, none of them the figure itself.** (a) **The definition did not move with it
   and must not** — §0.0000000b: the two definitions collapse into one, and what moved between
   $100,000 and $170,000 is the **cohort**, not the measure. (b) **D 2 refuses rather than trims**, so
   every trim-basis quantity in the record below is the wrong quantity for this text — §0.0000000c and
   §7 item 19. (c) **The figure was set off [MOO-48](/MOO/issues/MOO-48)'s rung-1 distribution**, and
   the identical-set interval around a ceiling on this definition has collapsed to **$4,114**, so if
   the window numeral `[12]` moves off rung 1 the distribution moves and the figure has to be
   re-walked. That coupling is unchanged and it is item 2. Also closed by the direction: MOO-46
   Decision 2(e)'s rule is retired as the ceiling's basis, which moots item 16.
   **Everything below is the revision-5 record, retained and superseded.**

   **The per-member ceiling figure — `[$100,000]` as of revision 5, and the warning about applying
   MOO-46's rule now cuts the other way.** [MOO-53](/MOO/issues/MOO-53) §4 settles two things and
   opens one. **Settled:** `[$80,000]` is unworkable — it is below **$81,278**, the cheapest election
   the bill admits, so it excludes part of the subsidy of **100 percent** of the eligible cohort, and
   it is not a close call (the same sweep at the zero calendar gap gives an 86 percent exclusion rate
   on the A+B cohort). And MOO-46 Decision 2(e)'s own rule returns **$100,000** on the A+B cohort
   **and** on the Pathway-A-only cohort — *"the two cohorts give the same figure, which is the
   strongest thing that can be said for it: it does not depend on whether MOO-49 confirms clause
   (v)."* So the bracket now holds the rule's answer. **Still open:** the rule is applied to a
   distribution that the **window numeral** (item 2) moves, and nobody has yet been asked what it
   returns at 6, 12, 18 and 24 months. **[MOO-48](/MOO/issues/MOO-48) is the issue that should answer
   it, and I could not reach it** — it is one of this issue's own blockers and cross-issue writes were
   refused while this issue is dependency-blocked, so the request is in
   [`revision5-handoff`](/MOO/issues/MOO-42#document-revision5-handoff) on this issue for you to route
   rather than in MOO-48's thread. **If it is already
   inside MOO-48's deadline-ladder scope, nothing needs doing.** **What is new and makes this harder than it was:** the ceiling has
   become a **sensitive** instrument. MOO-45's flat $30,000-wide identical-set band is gone, the
   identical-set interval around the figure has collapsed from $18,890 to **$4,114**, and every
   candidate figure now selects a materially different set of members. A figure chosen on the
   0-month distribution and left alone will not survive a window change the way $80,000 was expected
   to. **And state the cost of $100,000 when you carry it:** 69.1 percent of the bill-eligible cohort
   loses part of its subsidy and the worst-affected member faces **$70,343** of uncovered certified
   subsidy. If you would rather hold Pathway A whole, the figure is **$175,000** — it excludes nobody
   and does no fiscal work the model can see, and its only merit is that it bounds a salary-dispersion
   tail the model does not resolve.
4. **Keep the settlement redetermination in G 1, or cut it?** This is mine and I am flagging it
   rather than burying it. It is the most machinery revision 3 adds — three sentences — and it was
   not asked for. It exists because a window makes the certified subsidy indeterminate and every
   determinate alternative is worse (§6.1 item 11). What it buys: the cap reserves on the dearest
   case so it still binds; the Commonwealth pays on the fact rather than on an assumption; released
   excess funds other veterans; and in MOO-53's zero-acceleration case the subsidy collapses toward
   the price-discount floor instead of paying for an acceleration that did not occur. What it costs:
   a second actuarial determination per election, 90 days after each retirement, which VRS will price
   in the § 30-19.1:7 statement and may object to. **If you cut it, the honest fallback is to reserve
   and pay on the earliest-date valuation and to disclose the overstatement in the fiscal statement
   — not to leave the valuation date unnamed.**
5. **J 3's evaluation date — 1 December 2032 — and item 2 is now answered, so this is decidable.**
   At the 12-month window an election accepted in FY2032 is credited up to 12 months later and may
   retire up to 12 months after that, so **a FY2032 or FY2033 elector will not have retired by 1
   December 2032** and DPB would be evaluating a cohort that has not yet done the thing being
   evaluated. The three one-sentence options below are unchanged and the choice is still partly
   political, because 2033 is also the session that would decide whether to extend the pilot. **I have
   not touched the date.** *(Numbering: this is subsection J 3 since revision 10's renumbering; the
   heading's "K 3" is retained because the Director's own papers use it.)*
   At a 12- or 24-month window most FY2032 and FY2033 electors will not have retired by then, so DPB
   would evaluate a cohort that has not yet done the thing being evaluated. Three options, each one
   sentence: move the date; keep it and add a final evaluation tied to the last settlement; or keep it
   and label it an interim report in the text. The choice is partly political — whether you want an
   evaluation in front of the 2033 Session, which is also the session that would decide whether to
   extend the pilot — so it is yours, not mine. I have not touched the date.

   alternatives and the instruction block are gone. **Correction to what this item said in revisions 4

   resolved in favour of the smaller, published figure. Nothing further is open on S4 unless you take
   `[X2]` Alternative B, which deletes D 2 altogether.

9. **Disclose the exhaustion year and the denial rate, or move the figure — and the figures got
   worse in revision 5.** [MOO-53](/MOO/issues/MOO-53) §6.2 reprices this on the definition as
   drafted: the $3,000,000 funds **18.1** elections, is exhausted in **FY2030 — the first of four
   election years** — and denies **72.7 percent** of certified subsidy at central take-up. That
   supersedes MOO-45 §3.3's FY2031 and 58 percent, and it is the figure Fiscal & Impact Modeler says
   they *"would put in front of a money committee first."* The cause is revision 2's repair of the
   counterfactual, not a change of appropriation: fixing clause (ii) more than doubled the quantity
   the cap meters. **MOO-46 item 1 still changes the character of this question without closing it.**
   If $3,000,000 is the appropriation rather than a forecast, then exhaustion is what an appropriation
   does and the right answer is disclosure, not a larger number — but a brief that presents
   $3,000,000 as accommodating the design is now the thing a hostile witness will take apart within a
   sentence, because the design's own central-case demand is **$10,996,295, 3.67× the appropriation.**
   Both figures still move with item 2 and with item 15. **The decision I need is presentational, not
   arithmetical: does the denial rate go in the fiscal statement?** My recommendation is yes, with the
   two sentences that make it survivable — the cap is a rationing device by design, and the metered
   quantity **overstates true economic cost** because clause (ii) applies a population retirement rate
   to a self-selected group (MOO-53 §2.5; 14 percent of the cohort would have retired within a year
   anyway). Those two sentences together are the honest version. Neither can be said if the brief
   claims the appropriation accommodates the design.

   **One consequence for the Editor that is mine to flag, not to decide.** Everything written to date
   that describes the certified subsidy at **$49,860 per member** or **$4,930,054 aggregate** is on the
   superseded basis, and the current figures are **$111,211** and **$10,996,295**. In particular
   **nobody may describe the redraft as having reduced the subsidy** — it is 1.48× the double-counted
   figure it replaced, because repairing the counterfactual raised the number by more than removing the
   double count lowered it. Those are two separate changes that landed in one revision and they will
   be read as one unless the brief separates them.

14. **New, from [MOO-53](/MOO/issues/MOO-53) §2.4: should clause (ii) name the termination decrement?**
    One phrase, worth **−$558,151** aggregate and **−$5,645** per member — a 5.1 percent reduction in
    the quantity the cap meters. As drafted, clause (ii) applies the retirement and mortality rates and
    **no other decrement**, expressly. Including the valuation's termination (withdrawal) decrement is
    what Fiscal & Impact Modeler calls *"the actuary's reading"*, and VRS's actuary may well expect it.
    **The sign is the opposite of what a reader would guess, which is the reason this is yours and not
    mine:** for the 56 percent of this cohort already at 30 years of Plan 1 service, terminating
    commences an unreduced benefit **immediately** on less service, which *raises* the counterfactual
    present value — so the literal retirement-only reading I drafted is the **dearer** one for the
    Commonwealth. I held it because every priced figure in the package is on that basis and because a
    5 percent fiscal change should not arrive as a drafting preference. One phrase either way; nothing
    else in the bill moves with it.
15. **New, from [MOO-53](/MOO/issues/MOO-53) §7.1: the contingency is now inside the $3,000,000 — do
    you want it outside, and at what percentage?** Two coupled choices, both priced.
    - **Which side of the cap.** Revision 5 puts it **inside**: D 2 counts contingency amounts and D 3
      says they are charged against the limitation. The reason is structural rather than fiscal — a cap
      the act's own reserve can exceed is not a cap, and D 2's self-executing character is the whole of
      the agreed admission rule. **Inverting it is a request for appropriation**: at 25 percent the
      act would need **$3,750,000**, and at 10 percent **$3,300,000**. Keeping it inside costs
      elections instead: 18.1 → **16.2** at 10 percent, **14.7** at 20 percent, **14.0** at 25 percent.
      This is the first provision in the bill whose drafting makes an appropriation ask, which is why I
      took the version that does not make one.
    - **The percentage.** `[20]` as drafted. MOO-53 §7: 10 percent covers the median member and
      **fails the 90th percentile on every channel priced**; 20 percent covers the 90th; only 25
      percent covers the worst 95th (+21.0 percent). The supported range is **20–25 percent** and
      picking inside it is yours. **It is a lower bound** — the discount-rate channel could not be
      priced and the discount rate is the model's second-largest driver (±50bp = 31 percent of base).
      What I would not do is carry 10 percent, whose only argument was that it matched a
      recertification threshold revision 2 deleted.
    If the contingency is under-sized, D 6's consequence falls on the **member**: where the reserve is
    insufficient to fund an increase, the member must reduce the purchase, pay the increase, or cancel.
    That is the provision the percentage protects.

19. **PASS 7's F-25 IS THIS ITEM, INDEPENDENTLY FOUND (revision 24).** It reports that *"neither
    acceptance-stage refusal offers a reduction"* — D 2 refuses outright above the ceiling and
    subsection C refuses outright at zero, while D 6's reduce-or-cancel menu exists only
    post-acceptance — and records that *"the figure is the Director's; the absence of a reduction route
    is drafting."* **It does not change my recommendation**, which is against a partial-subsidy route:
    the zero case has nothing smaller to buy, and at the ceiling the member buys the excess at full
    actuarial cost, which reads as relief and operates as none. Revision 24 does give the refused member
    the **notice** she did not have — see pass 7's F-3 — so the member above the ceiling now at least
    learns the certified subsidy, her surviving rights and the standard cost. The original item follows.

    **NEW IN REVISION 9, and it is a consequence of unbracketing the ceiling rather than of the
    figure chosen. Does a member above the ceiling get nothing, or a partial subsidy?** As drafted,
    **nothing**: D 2's third sentence is *"The Board shall not accept an election for which the
    certified subsidy exceeds $170,000,"* and there is no lawful smaller purchase to fall back to,
    because subsection C fixes the quantity at the months actually needed and clause (vii)
    of "eligible member" requires the member to reach the unreduced date **with** the credit purchased.
    ` [Corrected in revision 18 —` [MOO-77](/MOO/issues/MOO-77) `F10.]`
    On [MOO-48](/MOO/issues/MOO-48)'s distribution that refuses roughly the top decile of the bill's
    own cohort outright. **Every ceiling figure in the priced record is a *trim* figure** — "69.1
    percent lose part of their subsidy," "worst uncovered amount $70,343," "$1,244,262 trimmed" — and
    none of those quantities describes this text. The modelling half is with Fiscal & Impact Modeler
    on [MOO-71](/MOO/issues/MOO-71). **The policy half is yours**, and it is one sentence either way:

    > <u>Where the certified subsidy for an election exceeds the amount stated in the third sentence
    > of this subdivision, the Board shall, upon the member's written request, accept the election and
    > reserve that amount, and the member shall pay, in addition to the amounts required by
    > subdivision B 4 of § 51.1-142.2, the amount by which the certified subsidy exceeds the amount so
    > reserved.</u>

    **I recommend against it** and I am saying so rather than drafting it in. It makes the member buy
    the excess at full actuarial cost, which for a member at the maximum of the distribution is about
    $39,000 on top of the pilot price — a figure most of this cohort cannot pay, so the provision would
    read as relief and operate as none. It also puts a means test into a veterans' bill by the back
    door, since who clears it depends on salary. **The honest alternative, if the top decile matters,
    is to raise the ceiling rather than to invent a partial subsidy** — and that is the Director's call
    on the same distribution §0.0000000b sets out. Refusal is also the cleaner story in a money
    committee: a bright line nobody negotiates around.

20. **NEW IN REVISION 12 — pass 4's S7. G 1's lesser-of is a one-way ratchet. Make it two-way, or
    keep the certainty?** This is the one of my five live findings that revision 12 **did not**
    draft, because it cannot be fixed without a decision. As drafted, G 1 settles *"the amount so
    redetermined or the amount last certified and reserved for the election, whichever is less."* So
    if the member's actual retirement proves **cheaper** than the reservation, the general fund keeps
    the saving; if it proves **dearer**, the amount settled is capped at the reservation and **the
    trust absorbs the overrun.** Pass 4 reads that against art. X, § 11 — the trust is held *"solely
    in the interests of the members"* — and it is attacking machinery I added in revision 3 and
    flagged as my own judgment in item 4, so the review did exactly what I asked it to.

    The two-way version is one clause: delete *"or the amount last certified and reserved for the
    election, whichever is less"* and add —

    > <u>and the Commonwealth shall pay the amount so redetermined, whether or not it exceeds the
    > amount last certified and reserved for the election.</u>

    **What each side actually costs.** Two-way is the fiduciarily clean answer and it is how a
    pension trust should be made whole, but it **breaks the cap**: $3,000,000 stops being a
    limitation on what the Commonwealth can be asked to pay, because an overrun at settlement is
    payable whatever the balance, and D 2's admission arithmetic no longer bounds the appropriation.
    One-way keeps the cap exact — which is the whole architecture MOO-35 directed and MOO-44 said the
    clause needed — at the price of a provision VRS's fiduciary counsel can object to. **A middle
    option exists and I would take it if you want one**: keep the lesser-of for the cap, and pay any
    overrun from the contingency amount reserved under D 3, which is already inside the
    $3,000,000 and already exists for exactly this class of surprise. That bounds the trust's
    exposure to the overruns the contingency cannot absorb rather than to all of them, and it
    changes no figure. **It is three options and they are all one or two sentences; none of them is
    a redraft, and I will draft whichever you pick.**

21. **NEW IN REVISION 16, and it is a drafting question rather than a policy one, but it is yours
    because the fix is a new sentence. Should the section say in terms which quantity "the service
    credit to be purchased" means?** Revision 16 needs two quantities — the amount the member
    **applies** to purchase, which is all that exists when the admission test in clause (vi) speaks,
    and the amount **to be purchased**, which the Board certifies and which every money provision
    consumes. §5.7 shows that all ten existing uses of the phrase speak after certification, so each
    is unambiguous today and the new clause (vi) names its own quantity expressly. **The section
    therefore works as drafted and I have added nothing.** But a reader who meets two similar
    phrases in one definition will ask whether the difference is deliberate, and one sentence in
    subsection A would answer him:

    > <u>"Service credit to be purchased" means, with respect to an election, the amount of service
    > credit stated in the Board's certification for that election under subsection D, as last
    > recertified under subdivision D 6.</u>

    **I recommend adopting it.** It costs a sentence, it strands nothing, and it converts the
    distinction clause (vi) relies on from an inference a reader has to draw into a definition he has
    to apply. I have **not** inserted it, because a new defined term reaches ten provisions the
    Director has not been asked about, and because a definition I add on my own judgment is exactly
    what the last several revisions of this document have been correcting.

    **And the process item behind it, which I am recording against myself.** The direction of
    11 October 22:02 was decided, unconditional and three minutes ahead of a revision, and it was
    neither drafted nor recorded as open — so for twelve store revisions this document's commentary
    cited clause numbers its own bill text did not contain. From here, **a decided direction I do not
    draft in the same revision goes into this section as a numbered item with the direction quoted
    verbatim**, so that an undrafted decision is visible as an undrafted decision. There is nothing
    for the Director to decide in this paragraph; it is here because §7 is where this draft keeps the
    things it would otherwise lose.

23. **NEW IN REVISION 18, from pass 5's m6. Narrow the involuntary-separation trigger, or leave it?**
    *"Involuntary separation"* is defined by reference to subsection B of § 2.2-3200, which uses the
    *"includes, but is not limited to"* formula. The trigger for keeping the pilot price on separation is
    therefore open-ended and, pass 5's point, **agency-controllable**: an agency and an employee who both
    want the outcome can characterise a departure to fit. The bill's own second sentence already excludes
    performance, misconduct and voluntary resignation. The narrowing sentence, drafted and **not**
    inserted:

    > <u>For the purposes of this section, "involuntary separation" includes only a separation of the
    > kinds enumerated in subsection B of § 2.2-3200.</u>

    **ANSWERED AND CLOSED — Policy Director, 12 October 2026: LEAVE IT. The narrowing sentence is not
    inserted and nothing in the bill changes** ([MOO-142](/MOO/issues/MOO-142), carrying [MOO-136](/MOO/issues/MOO-136)). Three
    reasons, for the record. **(1)** The arbitrage is already fenced twice: revision 10's middle treatment
    lets an involuntarily separated member keep the pilot price only by retiring in a qualifying retirement
    within 90 days, and this definition's own second sentence already excludes performance, misconduct and
    voluntary resignation. **(2)** The sentence would refuse the pilot price to a member whose separation is
    genuinely involuntary but not on the 1995 list — § 51.1-155.1 D's *"failure to obtain reappointment"*
    and an abolished position are the concrete cases — and the Workforce Transition Act's open formula
    exists because enumeration misses cases. **(3)** It would make this section's own residual dating limb
    inert, reintroducing by drafting exactly the empty-set defect the pass-15 record wrongly asserted.
    **This item was live either way**: it presupposed the open reading of § 2.2-3200 B, which [MOO-136](/MOO/issues/MOO-136)
    confirms at source is the correct one, so it is closed by this decision and **not** by that correction.
    **The reasoning below is retained as the record of the choice, not as a live question.**

    **I am neutral and I will draft either way.** It closes an arbitrage; it also refuses the pilot price
    to a member whose genuine involuntary separation happens not to be on the list, and the Workforce
    Transition Act's open formula exists because enumeration misses cases. Note that revision 10's middle
    treatment already narrowed this a great deal — an involuntarily separated member keeps the price only
    by retiring in a qualifying retirement within 90 days — so the arbitrage is worth much less than it
    was when the vesting was automatic.

24. **NEW IN REVISION 18, from pass 5's M7. Does the member get a remedy when the Department of Planning
    and Budget never answers?** Revision 18 takes pass 5's cheap half: at **90 days** pending, the Board
    must tell the applicant, state her § 51.1-142.2 B rights and state the standard cost as of that date.
    That is drafted. What is **not** drafted is any route by which the member's application can actually
    advance, because D 3's first sentence is a condition precedent — no confirmation, no reservation,
    ever. Two options, neither of which is a deeming rule:

    - **(a) Provisional reservation.** The Board may reserve on the lapse, the reservation being void if
      the Director refuses within a further fixed period. **This keeps DPB's veto and only changes who
      waits.** One sentence; I will draft it on instruction.
    - **(b) Deem the confirmation given.** The honest version of the thing you declined in
      [MOO-46](/MOO/issues/MOO-46) and which I declined to write in revision 12. It is appropriations
      control and it is not a drafting question.

    **Pass 5's own verdict on what revision 12 did here is the one to read:** *"it does not cure the
    stranding; it documents it."* Revision 18 documents it 90 days earlier and with a number the member
    can act on. That is all it does.

25. **NEW IN REVISION 18, from pass 5's M10. Item 2 is now answered, so this reads: the effective
    window is **15** months, not 12.** The numeral the Director chose is 12; the processing extension
    is available as of right for up to three months more; so the window the behavioural claim in the
    sponsor documents rests on is 12 and the window the statute delivers is up to 15. Nothing is
    repriced here and no figure moves, but the ladder rung the model priced is 12. *(Original
    statement:)* The effective window is
    `[12]` plus three months as of right, not `[12]`.** Pass 4's S12 required the three-month processing
    extension to be a **duty** rather than a discretion, and revision 12 drafted it that way — correctly,
    because a discretionary extension with no standard was the finding. But the standard is *"the Board's
    own processing requirements, or any other cause not attributable to the member"*, and the Board
    processes every retirement, so in the ordinary case a member who asks is **entitled**. Pass 5 verified
    that it does not compound: the cap is anchored to the base date, so repetition cannot stack past three
    months (§4.2), and revision 18's M 2 tolling runs only while a request is pending. **No figure moves
    and I have repriced nothing.** The item is here because the numeral you pick off MOO-48's ladder is
    effectively that numeral plus three, and because the behavioural claim in the sponsor documents is
    priced on the bracket alone. **If that is not what you intended, the fix is to make the extension
    discretionary again — which re-opens pass 4's S12 — or to shorten the three months.**

31. **NEW IN REVISION 21, RENUMBERED FROM 27 IN REVISION 24 (pass 8 F-16 found two items numbered
    27), AND SHARPENED BY PASS 8's F-6 FROM A SCHEDULE RISK INTO A COHORT-SELECTION RISK.**

    **Pass 8 re-ran the § 2.2-4002 verification independently and it holds** — subsection A exempts 26
    named bodies and subsection B exempts 24 categories of agency action, and neither list mentions
    retirement, pensions, employee benefits or benefit determinations. So the deletion is operative and
    the timetable problem below is real. **What pass 8 adds is who bears it.** Everything downstream is
    keyed to procedures that do not exist: *"complete application"* is **defined** by reference to them;
    the filing-date queue against the $3,000,000, D 4's completeness notice and the eligibility
    determination all run off the filing date of a *complete* application; and clauses (v), (vi) and
    (vii) of *"eligible member"* each measure **at the date the member files a complete application.**
    So a delay is not a delay in opening — it is a **cull**, and clause (v) makes it permanent: anyone
    who crosses her unreduced retirement eligibility date while the Board is drafting regulations is
    excluded **forever**. The cull is not random. It removes the members **closest to their unreduced
    date**, who are the **cheapest** subsidies in the book, so the surviving cohort's average certified
    subsidy rises against the $170,000 ceiling and the $3,000,000 buys fewer retirements than priced.
    **A nine-month delay costs nine months of a four-year pilot and makes what remains dearer per
    member.**

    **One more thing pass 8 establishes that I had not put in front of you: MOO-44 S12's decided answer
    was a 1 January 2029 adoption deadline, and the drafted text says 1 July 2029.** I moved it in
    revision 18 for a good reason — pass 5 found that a duty falling due on 1 January 2029 predates the
    first enactment's own effective date of 1 July 2029 — and that was tolerable while the exemption
    stood. Edit 8 made the deadline load-bearing, so the six months now matter. **I am not moving the
    date back, because it would re-create the pass-5 defect**, and I am recording the tension rather
    than resolving it by drafting.

    A **fourth cure** is therefore on the list, and it is the cheapest of the four: **(d) make
    enactment 5's permission a duty.** It currently reads that the Board *"may"* adopt the procedures
    before 1 July 2029 to take effect on that date if the first enactment is reenacted. *"Shall"*
    costs one word, creates no new rulemaking authority, and converts fourteen months of permitted lead
    time into fourteen months of required lead time. It does not close the gap between fourteen months
    and the 18-to-24-month estimate, so it is a complement to (b) rather than a substitute. **My
    recommendation is (b) plus (d).**

    **RETAINED RECORD — NEW IN REVISION 21, and it is a consequence of your Article 2 answer that my
    question did not put in front of you. The procedures now go through Article 2, and M 1 still
    requires them by 1 July 2029.** The deletion is done and it is operative — §5.11(b) verifies that § 2.2-4002
    exempts nobody in the retirement system, so silence subjects the procedures to the whole of
    Article 2. What my question card did not say is the timetable. The record on this issue is
    [MOO-44](/MOO/issues/MOO-44)'s **S12**, which is the finding that put the exemption in the draft
    in the first place: *ordinary Article 2 rulemaking takes 18 to 24 months from the 2028
    reenactment, so the program could not open on 1 July 2029.* I quoted the § 2.2-4006 C
    transparency step to you as the cost and did not restate S12's timetable. **That omission is
    mine.**

    The arithmetic, now that both decisions are in: the 2028 Session adjourns in about March and the
    reenactment is approved in about April 2028. Enactment 5 lets the Board adopt the procedures
    before 1 July 2029 to take effect on that date **if** the first enactment is reenacted — so the
    Board has roughly **14 months** for a notice of intended regulatory action, a proposed stage with
    its comment period, a final stage, executive review under § 2.2-4013 and legislative review under
    § 2.2-4014. That is inside the theoretical minimum and below the observed average. **The duty in
    M 1 is therefore a duty the Board may not be able to perform, and a duty with a deadline it
    cannot meet is a defect whichever way it is cured.**

    Three cures. **None is enacted**, because each is either a provision no document directs or a
    change to the pilot's calendar, and both are yours.

    - **(a) Leave it.** No text change. The Board starts in April 2028, probably misses 1 July 2029
      by some months, and the program opens late — with no provision saying what happens in the gap,
      because M 1 has no consequence clause and an application cannot be "complete" until the
      procedures define what complete means. **This is the option the text currently takes and I do
      not recommend it.**
    - **(b) Authorise emergency regulations.** One sentence in M 1: *"The Board may adopt the
      procedures required by this subsection as emergency regulations pursuant to § 2.2-4011."*
      § 2.2-4011 was retrieved for this note: subsection B's **automatic** route needs the law to
      require the regulation within **280 days or less** of enactment, which this act does not
      satisfy, so the sentence matters — it is what makes subsection A's route (Attorney General
      consultation, Governor's approval) available on the face of the statute rather than on an
      argument about what counts as an emergency. Subsection C caps an emergency regulation at **18
      months**, extendable by six, which covers 1 July 2029 to early 2031 while the full Article 2
      process runs behind it. **This is my recommendation**: it keeps your decision — the permanent
      rules go through Article 2, with executive and legislative review — and it keeps the opening
      date. It does not reinstate the exemption.
    - **(c) Move the dates.** Push M 1's deadline and enactment 2's effective date to 1 July 2030.
      Clean, and it costs a year of the pilot and moves every fiscal-year figure in the package.
      **Not mine to choose.**

    One thing that is true on all three options and belongs in the fiscal statement either way:
    Article 2 rulemaking is a real administrative cost on VRS and it will appear in the
    § 30-19.1:7 statement. It was not in the costing, because the costing was done against a draft
    that carried the exemption.

    **ADDED IN STORE REVISION 33, from [MOO-95](/MOO/issues/MOO-95)'s pass 9, finding M-12 — and it is a
    correction to the premise of this item, not an addition to it.** Pass 8's verification, which this
    item relies on, was that *"subsection A exempts 26 named bodies and subsection B exempts 24
    categories of agency action, and neither list mentions retirement, pensions, employee benefits or
    benefit determinations."* Pass 9 re-ran that count on the version now in force and **it reproduces
    exactly** — 26 and 24, no mention of retirement. **But it is the wrong test.** Subsection B does not
    exempt by programme name; it exempts by the **subject of the action**, and subdivision **B 4** reads
    *"Grants of state or federal funds or property."* Subsection N makes the certified subsidy a payment
    of state money from an appropriation made for that purpose, so the Board's act of reserving,
    refusing or releasing it is arguably an action relating to a grant of state funds — and subsection B
    carries **no** carve-back to §§ 2.2-4024, 2.2-4030 and 2.2-4031, so if it bites it removes the whole
    chapter. **Pass 9 thinks the argument loses on balance and so do I**: § 51.1-124.11 shows the General
    Assembly legislating APA process and Article 5 review for a VRS benefit determination, which is poor
    company for a grants exemption; the member receives no grant, but service credit at a price, and the
    money goes to the trust; and B 4's settled use is discretionary grant programmes. **But a
    well-resourced respondent will plead it, and no document of ours may say that § 2.2-4002 has been
    cleared** — what was cleared is that the lists do not say "retirement." It makes the timetable
    problem in this item *smaller*, not larger, and it makes item 32's sentence more worth taking, not
    less. Pass 9 has nothing to add to **(b) plus (d)**, which remains my recommendation.


---

32. **NEW IN REVISION 24, from pass 8's F-7, and it is the one place where silence defeats your own
    answer. Should the act say expressly that the subsection M procedures go through Article 2?**
    Your answer of 12 October was to drop the exemption entirely, and my own record of why reads *"the
    permanent rules go through Article 2, with executive and legislative review."* **Silence does not
    deliver that.** Pass 8 read § 2.2-4006 A's eighteen subdivisions and found at subdivision 2
    *"regulations establishing agency organization, internal practice or procedures, including
    delegations of authority"* — which is available to the Board, and much of M 1's commissioned list
    reads like internal practice: *"the determination of initial average compensation where records are
    incomplete,"* *"the determination and reservation of certified subsidies and contingency amounts,"*
    *"recertification,"* *"the crediting of service."*

    **The failure case is the Board taking that route for the whole of M 1 — including the content of a
    complete application, which is the single most rights-affecting rule in the scheme because the queue
    against the $3,000,000 is ordered by the filing date of a *complete* application.** No notice of
    intended regulatory action, no 60-day comment period, no executive or legislative review, and a
    refused applicant with no rulemaking record to challenge. The Commonwealth would have **the delay
    and lose the review** — the worst of both, achieved lawfully, under item 31's timetable pressure.

    The sentence, drafted and **not** inserted: *"The Board shall adopt the procedures required by this
    subsection in accordance with Article 2 (§ 2.2-4006 et seq.) of the Administrative Process Act."*

    **I recommend taking it, and I have not inserted it, for a reason I want on the record.** Your
    decision was a deletion; this is an insertion, and my standing rule is that a provision no cited
    document directs goes into this section rather than into the bill. It also has a real cost: it
    forecloses the § 2.2-4006 A 2 route for provisions that are *genuinely* internal practice, which is
    a slower Board. There is a narrower version if you prefer — apply Article 2 expressly to *"the
    content of a complete application and the waiting list"* and leave the rest to the default — and it
    is three words longer. **One sentence either way, inside M 1, with no other provision touched.**

    **ADDED IN STORE REVISION 33.** [MOO-95](/MOO/issues/MOO-95)'s pass 9 verified § 2.2-4006 A 2
    verbatim — *"[r]egulations that establish or prescribe agency organization, internal practice or
    procedures, including delegations of authority"* — and confirms that this item's failure case is
    correctly stated and that the Article 2 and Article 3 citation forms used here are right.
    **It recommends taking the sentence, and its M-12 finding (item 31 above) strengthens rather than
    weakens that recommendation**: if § 2.2-4002 B 4 is even arguable, the sentence stops being a
    confirmation of an Article 2 obligation and becomes the thing that **creates** it, which is worth
    more and costs the same.

33. **NEW IN REVISION 24, from pass 7's F-20. Nothing says whether a determination under this section is
    an Administrative Process Act "case decision," so M 2's saving clause may save a right that does not
    exist.** M 2 ends *"Nothing in this subsection limits the right … to judicial review pursuant to
    Article 5 (§ 2.2-4025 et seq.)."* Pass 7 checked § 2.2-4025 and VRS is **not** excluded from Article
    5, so the clause is not void at the agency level. But Article 5 review runs to a **case decision**,
    and the bill never characterises its determinations as such and never provides for the Article 3
    proceeding in which a case decision is ordinarily made. **Failure case:** a member refused under D 4
    petitions in Richmond Circuit Court; VRS moves to dismiss on the ground that the determination is
    *"solely the internal management or routine of an agency"* under § 2.2-4025 A (ii), that no Article
    3 proceeding was held or required, and that there is no case decision to review. Either way **the
    member who was promised review litigates about whether review exists** — and revision 24 has just
    added five more determinations carrying an express M 2 review right, so the clause is doing more
    work than it was.

    **MY RECOMMENDATION IS WITHDRAWN IN STORE REVISION 33, and the substitute is better.** The
    candidate text I had drafted and recommended was: *"A determination of the Board under this section
    is a case decision for the purposes of the Administrative Process Act, and the written
    reconsideration provided by this subsection constitutes the proceeding required by Article 3
    (§ 2.2-4018 et seq.) of that Act."* **Do not take it.**
    [MOO-95](/MOO/issues/MOO-95)'s pass 9, finding S-9, verified § 2.2-4021 B from source: in any
    informal fact-finding, formal proceeding or summary case decision proceeding in which a hearing
    officer is not used, the agency must decide **within 90 days**, and *"[i]f the agency does not render
    a decision within 90 days, the named party … may provide written notice to the agency that a
    decision is due. If no decision is made within 30 days from agency receipt of the notice, **the
    decision shall be deemed to be in favor of the named party**."* The three named exceptions are
    environmental boards; VRS is not among them, and § 2.2-4018's seven Article 3 exemptions do not reach
    retirement determinations. **So my sentence would have imported a statutory default judgment against
    the Board.** A member refused because her certified subsidy exceeds $170,000 requests
    reconsideration, the Board is silent, she serves the § 2.2-4021 B notice, and thirty days later her
    reservation exists by operation of law — against limitations D 2 declares *"self-executing and
    requir[ing] no further act of the General Assembly, the Board, or any other person."* **Those two
    cannot both be true**, and the route costs the applicant one letter.

    **The substitute, drafted and not inserted, and it is the Code's own formula.** § 51.1-124.11
    already contains the General Assembly's words for a VRS benefit determination, verified verbatim by
    pass 9: notice, *"an opportunity to be heard in accordance with the provisions of the Administrative
    Process Act,"* and an express entitlement to *"judicial review pursuant to Article 5 (§ 2.2-4025 et
    seq.)."* Adapted to M 2:

    > <u>The Board shall give a member, retiree, or beneficiary adversely affected by a determination
    > under this section reasonable prior written notice and an opportunity to be heard in accordance
    > with the Administrative Process Act (§ 2.2-4000 et seq.), and any member, retiree, or beneficiary
    > aggrieved by such a determination shall be entitled to judicial review pursuant to Article 5
    > (§ 2.2-4025 et seq.) of that Act.</u>

    **This is now my recommendation.** It gives the member a real and express review right, it is
    drafted in Title 51.1 for this agency and has been on the books since 1998, and it does **not**
    characterise anything as a case decision or engage § 2.2-4021's default. The one gap, stated
    honestly: it does not foreclose a § 2.2-4025 A (ii) *"internal management or routine"* argument —
    **but neither did the sentence I withdrew**, because that argument goes to the character of the
    action and not to the label a statute puts on it. If you would rather not legislate review at all,
    the alternative is still to delete M 2's last sentence and say nothing, which is honest and gives the
    member nothing.

34. **MOVED TO THE RETAINED RECORD IN STORE REVISION 58 — this item is closed and is not an open question. The whole of it is at [`bill-text-s8-revision-history-2`](/MOO/issues/MOO-42#document-bill-text-s8-revision-history-2), where a reference of the form §7 item 34 still resolves.** Its own statement of what was decided is retained here verbatim; where that statement refers to reasoning *"below"*, the reasoning is in that document and not here.

   **ANSWERED 12 OCTOBER 2026 — ALL FIVE ENACTED IN STORE REVISION 44, THREE OF THEM WITH A REPAIR
    [MOO-121](/MOO/issues/MOO-121) FOUND IN THE DRAFTED WORDS.** The commission was extended to M 2, D 7,
    D 2 and D 6 for this purpose only, and the five strings as enacted are at
    [`moo101-cut-record`](/MOO/issues/MOO-42#document-moo101-cut-record) §2, each with the anchor it was
    verified unique against. **Nothing in this item is open.**

   **Pointers it carried:** [MOO-121](/MOO/issues/MOO-121); [`moo101-cut-record`](/MOO/issues/MOO-42#document-moo101-cut-record); [MOO-102](/MOO/issues/MOO-102); item 76; item 75; item 77; item 2

35. **NEW IN REVISION 30. [MOO-87](/MOO/issues/MOO-87)'s option (c) has made subsection C's zero bar
    unreachable. Should it come out, and does clause (v) of *"eligible member"* come out with it?** This
    is a question, not a tidy-up. The verification is at **§0.x f** of revision 30's record: clause (ii)
    of subsection C is now measured *"as of the filing date of the member's complete application"* and
    clause **(v)** requires that the member *"has not reached his unreduced retirement eligibility date
    … on the date the member files a complete application."* **Same date, same basis, and one-way
    containment** — *not* "exact complements," which is how store revision 32 put it and which pass
    10's Mi-12 corrects: membership service was a **subset** of creditable service, so clause (ii) = 0
    always implied clause (v) failed, but the converse did not hold. The direction this item relies on
    is the one that always held, and **store revision 34's repair of the clause (ii) base closes the gap
    in fact as well** (§7 item 43). If clause (ii) is zero she has reached that date, so clause (v)
    fails, so she is not an eligible member — and subsection C opens *"An **eligible member** may purchase."* **For every
    person subsection C addresses, clause (ii) is greater than zero.** The bar cannot fire; R3's notice
    duty cannot be triggered; and the member it was written for is refused under subdivision **D 4**,
    where she gets more than R3 gave her.

    > **MEASURED, AND IT IS NOT THE GROUND. NEW FROM [MOO-32](/MOO/issues/MOO-32), routed here by the chart work on 12 October 2026.** On the authorised cohort the zero bar refuses **0.0000 of 19.6304 members**, and the set it would refuse is a **strict subset** of clause (vii)'s no-advance band (1.014822) — so with clause (vii) in the text the bar removes nobody clause (vii) has not already removed. On the revision 18 text, *without* clause (vii), it refused **0.4631** (`moo32_v6_basis.py` §0, locked against MOO-78's own figures before reporting). **Three consequences.** First, this **corroborates option (b) and does not found it**: this item rests on clause (v) containment, which holds for every population rather than on a count, so Fiscal's caveat — *"it is a property of this population, not a theorem about the statute, and I would not draft language that depends on it staying true"* — is answered. No option here depends on the count staying at zero. Second, the 0.4631 → 0.0000 move has **two independent causes, either of which empties the bar alone**: clause (vii), and store revision 32's round-up of clause (ii), which turns a sub-month need into one month instead of zero. Third, **option (a)'s one remaining argument is now measured and empty** — the bar is not belt-and-braces against a cohort shift, because on any cohort clause (v) and clause (vii) each reach the member before it does, and [MOO-87](/MOO/issues/MOO-87)'s earlier statement that the bar *"catches the sliver where the date falls inside the filing month"* is **withdrawn**: round-up took that sliver. **The recommendation does not change and no figure moves on any of the three options**; no chart in the MOO-32 set implies the bar rations anything. **AND THE CALL HAS BEEN HANDED TO ME**: the Policy Director's routing of 12 October 2026 reads *"it is a drafting question, not a modelling one, and I am not reaching into the text — this is the fact, and the call is yours."* **I take option (b)**, which is enacted in **store revision 38** — not, as this paragraph first said, in the revision that drafted out [MOO-107](/MOO/issues/MOO-107)'s pass 11, which landed as store revision 36 without the enactment — and sent to the next bounded counsel pass with the three checks above named. One word from the Director reverses this and the bar goes back.

    **OPTION (b) IS TAKEN AND ENACTED IN STORE REVISION 38, AND THE BAR IS OUT OF THE TEXT.**
    The call was handed to me in terms by the Policy Director on 12 October 2026 — *"it is a
    drafting question, not a modelling one, and I am not reaching into the text — this is the fact,
    and the call is yours."* Subsection C's zero bar and its refusal notice are **deleted**; clause
    **(v)** stays and is now the sole screen for the fact; clause **(vii)** is untouched. **No figure
    moves** — the participant count is identical on all three options, because the admitted
    population is fixed by clauses (v) to (viii) on every one of them. The ground is **clause (v)
    containment, which holds on any population**, not the measured count; see the annotation above for
    why the count corroborates the choice rather than founding it. The deletion goes to the next
    bounded counsel pass **as its own item**, with the three checks below named, because deleting an
    enacted provision is not a tidy-up. **One word from the Director reverses this and the bar goes
    back**, and §7 item 26 re-opens with it. Options (a) and (c) are retained below as the record of
    what was weighed, and the three checks under (b) are retained because the next pass must re-run
    them against the deletion as landed.

    - **(a) Leave both.** No text change, and **nothing is broken on this option** — the sentence is
      sound as repaired. The cost is that a reader must reconcile a bar that cannot fire with a
      definition that makes it unnecessary, and a Division of Legislative Services drafter will ask why
      the bill screens one fact twice.
    - **(b) Delete the bar and its notice; keep clause (v).** Clause (v) is the operative screen and it
      sits in the admission test with every other eligibility fact.

      > In subsection C, delete <u>"The Board shall not accept an election for which the amount
      > determined under clause (ii) of this subsection is zero."</u> and delete the sentence beginning
      > <u>"Where the amount determined under clause (ii) of this subsection is zero."</u>

      **Three things must be checked before this is enacted and I have checked all three.** **CORRECTED IN
      STORE REVISION 34, from pass 10's M-5: subdivision D 1 does *not* require the certification to
      state the clause (ii) amount.** Its list says *"the amount of service credit to be purchased,"*
      which is the elected quantity. The duty to state the clause (ii) amount is **subsection C's own
      second sentence**, and **store revision 48's edit 1 took the substituted determination out of that sentence's period, which is pass 16's S-1; store revision 53 gives it its own 30-day period** — so the
      conclusion stands and the premise was wrong: neither deletion touches that sentence. The
      corrected enumeration of the nine consumers of clause (ii) and of subsection C is at **§5.16**,
      and **none of the nine consumes the bar**, so nothing is stranded. And the **post-acceptance** zero case is not
      left unhandled: **subdivision D 6's fourth sentence already governs it in terms** — see item 34
      item 5, which is the repair that provision needs whichever way you answer here.
    - **(c) Delete clause (v) and keep the bar.** **I do not recommend it.** It moves an eligibility
      fact out of the definition that collects them, and it screens later — at certification rather than
      at the eligibility determination — which costs the member thirty days and the Board a reservation
      it must then release.

    **Whichever way you answer, the bar, clause (v) and clauses (vi) to (viii) must be amended together
    or not at all**, which is why this is one item and not two. <u>[Store revision 46: this paragraph read
    *"the bar and clause (v)"* and so contradicted the wider statement this item makes below; widened on
    pass 14's **S-2**, which found the inconsistency.]</u> **The participant count is identical on all three options**,
    because the admitted population is fixed by clauses (v) to (viii) on every one of them; the only
    question is which provision does the work. **If you take (b), §7 item 26 closes with it.**

    **PASS 13 HAS NOW REVIEWED THE DELETION AND DOES NOT RECOMMEND REVERSING IT — but it defeats this item's stated ground, and store revision 41 repairs the ground in the text.**
    [MOO-119](/MOO/issues/MOO-119) returned **12 findings, 0 fatal**, and says in terms that nothing in it is a basis for reversal. Four things in this item are corrected rather than left standing.

    - **The sentence *"For every person subsection C addresses, clause (ii) is greater than zero"* is a dichotomy that does not cover one measured class, and it is withdrawn as stated.** For a Plan 1 member under age 50 at the filing date, no number of months of creditable service answers clause (ii) — § 51.1-153 B 2 requires age 50 **and** 30 years, and service cannot buy age — so clause (ii) is neither zero nor greater than zero, and clause (v) passes. **0.0826 participants, 0.0259 of them admitted, at $145,396 to $166,226 each.** **Store revision 41's new third sentence of subsection C closes it**: where no number of months would answer clause (ii), the limb does not limit, and the Board must state that in the certification. The proposition as now true reads: **for every person subsection C addresses, clause (ii) either exceeds zero or does not limit her, and the Board must say which in writing.**
    - **Clause (vi) is co-load-bearing with clause (v), and the protection rule now names it.** **Whichever way you answer, the bar, clause (v) and clauses (vi) to (viii) must be amended together or not at all.** Clause (v) is a date test the age-constrained member passes; clause (vi)'s one-month reachability window is what confines her class. A later revision that widens clause (vi) widens that class with nothing downstream to catch it.
    - **The ground of the containment is § 51.1-124.3, and this item should have cited it.** *"Creditable service"* is defined as prior service *"plus membership service for which credit is **allowable**"* — allowable, not credited — so the chapter's own defined term is entitlement-based in both limbs, which is a stronger ground than §0.x b's unsourced *"same basis"* (pass 13's F-11). **Store revision 41's second edit puts the same base on the face of clause (v)** in clause (ii)'s own words, so the construction no longer has to be inferred from an asymmetry.
    - **One serious finding is live and it is not about this item's answer.** Pass 13's F-3 — an accepted election with nothing to buy has no defined downstream operation — is **§7 item 70**, with the reviewer's own recommended sentence corrected because it does not reach the two post-acceptance routes it is offered against.

    **ANSWERED 12 OCTOBER 2026 — (a) GOVERNS. BOTH DELETED SENTENCES ARE BACK IN SUBSECTION C, VERBATIM,
    IN STORE REVISION 44.** The Director was asked the conflict in terms — MOO-101's commission answers
    this item **(a)** and directs *"[d]o not delete subsection C's zero bar in this revision"*, while
    store revision 38 enacted **(b)** on the Director's later routing on
    [MOO-87](/MOO/issues/MOO-87) — and answered: *"(a) governs — put both deleted sentences back into
    subsection C verbatim, re-open §7 item 26 in the form it already carries, and cut MOO-98's M1 in
    form (B)."* All three limbs are performed in that revision.

    - **What went back, verbatim, in its original position as the third and fourth sentences:** the bar —
      *"The Board shall not accept an election for which the amount determined under clause (ii) of this
      subsection is zero."* — and the refusal notice it triggers, in the form it had immediately before
      the deletion (store revision 37), which is to say with the standard-cost limb store revision 24
      added and the **fact-keyed** trigger and *"30 days after the member's application is completed"*
      clock store revision 30 gave it. Because § 51.1-142.4 is a wholly new section the restoration
      shows no markup: the words are simply present again.
    - **No figure moves, and the Director knew that when answering.** The participant count is identical
      on all three options; MOO-32 measured the bar as refusing **0.0000 of 19.6304**. The honest
      statement of what the restoration buys is **not** a refusal that would otherwise be missed: it is a
      **named, reviewable notice** for a case the rest of the section reaches only through subdivision
      D 4, plus a referent for the cross-reference at item 75 below.
    - **It does not collide with store revision 41's new third sentence, and the two are complementary.**
      The bar fires where the clause (ii) amount **is zero**; revision 41's sentence governs where clause
      (ii) **has no value at all** and does not limit. A member cannot be in both classes, nothing in
      either sentence refers to the other, and revision 41's record is undisturbed. The proposition this
      item now rests on is the corrected one: **for every person subsection C addresses, clause (ii)
      either exceeds zero, or does not limit her — and the Board must say which, in writing.**
    - **What the restoration does not do.** It does not make the bar reachable. On the ground stated in
      store revision 38's record paragraph b, and on §5.20's Check 2, clause (v) of *"eligible member"*
      screens out the member whose clause (ii) is zero before subsection C opens. **The restored bar is a
      belt beside a brace**, and that is the reason store revision 38 took it out; the Director's answer
      is that the belt stays. Nothing in that assessment is withdrawn and no measurement changes.


36. **NEW IN REVISION 32, from [MOO-93](/MOO/issues/MOO-93)'s pass 9, finding S-2, and it is the one place where option (c) is not yet on
    the basis it was priced on.** Clause (ii) is measured *"as of the filing date of the member's
    complete application."* Subdivision D 4 then provides that an application cured within the 60-day
    window **retains the filing date of the original application for all purposes of this section** —
    so the date that governs clause (ii) is the date the **original**, possibly near-empty, application
    was filed, which can be up to **90 days earlier** (30 days for the Board's completeness notice, 60
    to cure). Nothing in the bill requires an application to contain anything in order to take a filing
    date, and the definition of *"complete application"* forbids the Board from requiring by procedure
    *"any item that is not necessary"*, so the Board cannot raise the floor and cannot refuse the date.
    **Revision 22 converted the cure window into a quantity lever.** Under the old completion-date
    measure, filing early bought queue position and nothing else. Pass 9 measured it: at the D 4
    maximum the mean certified quantity rises **19.89 → 22.74 months (+14.3 percent)**, zero-bar
    refusals go to **0.00** and refusals at the $170,000 ceiling rise **1.23 → 1.46**. Option (c) was
    priced on `moo78_screen.basis_cells(R18, measure_years=0.0)` — on the assumption that the complete
    application is in hand.
    - **(a) Leave it.** Coherent, and it has an argument: eligibility under D 4 and quantity under
      clause (ii) then run on exactly the same date, which is what option (c) was for. The cost is a
      lever available to an advised applicant and not to an unadvised one — the same objection that
      defeated subsection C's early-completion sentence, re-entering by another door.
    - **(b) Move both dates together, keeping queue order where it is. My recommendation.** One
      sentence in subdivision D 4, after the two retention sentences:

      > <u>Where an application is completed after the date on which it was originally filed, the filing
      > date of the applicant's complete application is, for the purposes of the determination of
      > eligibility required by this subdivision and of every determination under subsection C, the
      > date on which the application becomes complete; and the date on which the application was
      > originally filed governs the order in which the Board accepts elections under this subdivision
      > and the order of the waiting list under subdivisions 3 and 5.</u>

      Queue position and measurement do different jobs and nothing requires them to be the same date.
      **This interacts with item 34 item 1**: if M 2 reconsideration is given filing-date protection on
      the F3 pattern, it should protect the original filing date for queue order and the completeness
      date for measurement, which the sentence above already distinguishes.
    - **(c) Pass 9's own direction — move clause (ii) only. I am not drafting it and I would advise
      against it.** It leaves eligibility (clauses (v), (vi) and (vii)) tested on the retained filing
      date while quantity is measured up to 90 days later, which is the divergence between admission
      and quantity that option (c) exists to remove and that revision 17 was written to close. It would
      also break the exact complement that **§7 item 35** rests on. If the dates move, they move
      together.

    **A note for Fiscal on any answer here — CORRECTED IN STORE REVISION 34, from pass 10's S-2, because
    as written it was not executable.** Revision 32's rounding edit and whichever of these options is
    taken do move the same quantity in the same direction, but they have **opposite signs on the
    subsidy**: the marginal month of clause (ii) credit is close to subsidy-neutral and cap utilisation
    moves **−0.08 points** on the rounding, while moving the measurement date moves the member further
    from her date, grows the acceleration and took utilisation **79.3 → 80.9 percent** at d = −1. A
    combined run would also report the rounding contribution as **zero**, because the cohort carries no
    fractional needs at all (§0.x c). **Measure them in two runs, or decompose.**

    **ADDED IN STORE REVISION 34, from pass 10's M-6 — if you take option (b), it defeats store revision
    32's edit 3 unless you conform it.** Option (b)'s sentence redirects the filing date *"for the
    purposes of the determination of eligibility required by this subdivision and of every determination
    under subsection C"* — and the certification duty that edit 3 added is in subdivision **D 1**, which
    is in subsection D and not in subsection C. The certification would then state the **retained
    original** filing date beside a clause (ii) amount computed as of the completeness date, and the
    member checking the arithmetic — which is the whole reason edit 3 exists — would find it does not
    reconcile. **The fix is one of two:** extend option (b)'s redirect to subsection D's certification
    duty, or have D 1 state *"the date as of which the amounts under subsection C were determined."*
    Either is a word or two and neither is a new rule. **This is a condition on option (b), not a defect
    in edit 3.**

    **ADDED IN STORE REVISION 33, from [MOO-95](/MOO/issues/MOO-95)'s pass 9, finding M-9 — the same
    question from the other end, and the reason I did not enact its fix.** Pass 9 found that D 4's new
    limb gives the applicant 60 days from a *late* completeness notice and preserves her filing date,
    *"and the Board shall state every such delay in the report required by subsection J"* — and that if
    the Board never gives the notice at all she has date-retention and nothing else, because **there is
    no deemed-complete rule.** The APA's own model, § 2.2-4021 B, runs the other way: the agency's
    silence decides against it. Its drafted fix is *"[w]here the Board does not give the notice required
    by this subdivision within 30 days after the application is filed, the application shall be deemed
    complete as of its filing date."*

    **I have not enacted it, and the reason is this item.** A deemed-complete rule lets an application
    that was **in fact incomplete** fix the date on which clause (ii) of subsection C measures the
    quantity — which is exactly the lever this item is about, except that it opens it by operation of law
    rather than by a cure. If you take option **(b)** above, the two answers are consistent: queue order
    keeps the original filing date, measurement moves to the date the application becomes complete, and
    the deemed-complete rule can then be added safely because a deemed-complete application *is* a
    complete application for both purposes. **If you take option (a), do not add it** — it would measure
    a real quantity on an application that did not contain the facts needed to measure it. The loss pass
    9 is worried about is real either way: its scenario has an applicant file on day two of the pilot,
    wait eleven months for a completeness notice, retain her filing date into a queue that has already
    moved, and — if she crossed her unreduced retirement eligibility date meanwhile — be excluded
    **permanently** by clause (v). That is the strongest argument for (b) in the file.

37. **NEW IN REVISION 32, from [MOO-93](/MOO/issues/MOO-93)'s pass 9, finding S-3 — the most serious finding in the pass, and what it says
    is that the Director's stated purpose for the bar is not being delivered.** *"Eligible member"*
    status, including the new clause (vii), is determined **once**, under subdivision D 4, as of the
    filing date, and nothing in the bill re-tests it — not at acceptance, not at recertification, not at
    payment, not at crediting. Revision 22 froze clause (ii) on the same date. The interval between
    that date and the crediting date can run **well past a year**: D 3's 30 days for the Department of
    Planning and Budget, its 90-day lapse notice, the fresh fiscal-year confirmation during which *"the
    Board shall accept no further election"*, D 5's waiting list running to expiry, subsection E's 12
    months to pay, and 30 days to credit. Pass 9's case: a member files 20 months short in June 2030
    and is credited 20 months in December 2031, when she was **2 months** short on her own accrual —
    18 months of subsidised credit that accelerate nothing, with the Commonwealth paying the certified
    subsidy on all 20 under subsection N. Clause (vii) was satisfied in June 2030 and is never asked
    again; clause (viii) does not catch her (§0.x c item 2); the zero bar does not catch her. **The
    repealed completion-date measure did catch her.** Pass 9 also records cap utilisation under (c) at
    **132.2 percent** at high take-up, so D 5 wait-listing is structural rather than exceptional and a
    wait-listed member can be measured on facts three years stale.
    - **(a) Leave it and disclose the leakage.** The honest version of this option is a line in the
      fiscal statement and in the memo's risk section, not silence. Pass 9 has already directed the
      Editor to carry it.
    - **(b) Re-test clause (vii) at acceptance. My recommendation, and it keeps your condition
      exactly** — you required the bar to operate *"at admission, not after payment"*, and acceptance
      **is** admission. A new sentence at the end of subdivision D 4:

      > <u>Before accepting an election the Board shall determine again, as of the date on which it
      > accepts the election, whether the applicant satisfies clause (vii) of the definition of
      > "eligible member" in subsection A, and shall not accept the election where the applicant does
      > not satisfy that clause as of that date. Where the Board determines that it may not accept an
      > election for that reason, the Board shall give the applicant the notice this subdivision
      > requires where the Board determines that an applicant is not an eligible member, no later than
      > 30 days after making the determination, and the applicant may seek review of the determination
      > under subsection M.</u>

      This removes members and therefore moves a count, which is why it is yours and not mine. It is
      also the only one of the three options that reaches the wait-listed member.
    - **(c) Re-test clause (ii) as well, at acceptance.** Add, after *"does not satisfy that clause as
      of that date"*: <u>", and shall determine again, as of that date, the amount under clause (ii) of
      subsection C"</u>. **I do not recommend it.** It restores the minimum-necessary rule at
      admission, but it shrinks every member's certified quantity by the whole pendency, and it is
      option (b) of item 22 re-entering through subdivision D 4: the size of the benefit would again
      depend on how long the Department of Planning and Budget took, which is the defect option (c) was
      chosen to remove.

38. **NEW IN REVISION 32, from [MOO-93](/MOO/issues/MOO-93)'s pass 9, finding M-5. It is notice content rather than policy — but it is an
    addition, and additions are yours.** Subdivision D 4 requires a refusal notice to state *"that the
    applicant may file a further application."* For a clause (vii) refusal that is almost always false.
    Clause (vii) fails when the maximum purchasable amount cannot advance her date by a month; the
    maximum purchasable amount can only grow if the limb that binds it grows; and Fiscal's
    `out_moo78_mpa_limbs.csv` records the **B 2 entitlement** limb binding for **100 percent** of the
    cohort at every take-up level — military service is fixed, so it never grows, while the
    without-purchase date only gets closer. A veteran refused in March 2030 who refiles in March 2031
    on the Board's own written invitation is refused again on worse facts, having paid for the records
    the Board required. **Drafted, not enacted**, as an addition to D 4's notice sentence after *"and
    shall state that the applicant may file a further application"*:

    > <u>and, where the determination is made by reason of clause (vii) of that definition, shall state
    > the effect of that clause on a further application</u>

    One clause, no new duty of substance, and it stops the notice reading as a remedy when it is a
    courtesy. The alternative is to say nothing and let the veterans' service organisations say it for
    us; pass 9 quotes them doing exactly that.

39. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 33, from [MOO-95](/MOO/issues/MOO-95)'s pass 9, finding S-2. The retirement
    window now has an outer bound, and the numeral in it is mine, not yours.** I told you in §6.0-24 that
    the window has *"four bounded components, each bounded on the face of the text."* **Three were.** D 1
    extended the window *"by the number of days by which the statement or notice is late"* with no cap: a
    statement 400 days late lengthened the window by 400 days, and **a statement never given left it
    open for ever**, in which case the member can never be a member who *"does not retire in a qualifying
    retirement"*, so he retires years later, his retirement is a **qualifying** retirement, and the
    Commonwealth settles a subsidy for an acceleration that is **negative**. G 1's ratchet protects the
    trust and not the appropriation, and the reservation sits charged against the $3,000,000 throughout.

    **What I enacted**, in the definition of *"final qualifying retirement date"*:

    > <u>In no event shall a member's final qualifying retirement date, as extended under subdivision
    > D 1, be later than 12 months after the date determined under the first two sentences of this
    > definition.</u>

    **Why 12 and not something else.** It is **your own window length**, so the Code gains no figure
    nobody has seen, and capping the fourth component rather than the total leaves the other three
    reading exactly as you decided them. The envelope the text now delivers is **12 months + 3 months +
    120 days + at most 12 months**. Two tighter alternatives, each a single edit and each drafted:

    - **(a) Pass 9's preference — an absolute bound.** *"In no event shall a member's final qualifying
      retirement date be later than 21 months after the member's earliest qualifying retirement date."*
      It bounds all four components in one sentence, which is the tidiest drafting. It introduces a
      figure (21) that no document has priced, and it can cut into the three-month processing extension
      and the 120 days of tolling you have already decided — that is, it can take from the member a
      period another provision gives her.
    - **(b) A short cap on the lateness alone.** *"…shall not exceed 90 days in the aggregate."* Tightest
      of the three and the most protective of the appropriation. It is also the one that most often
      leaves a member who was told late with less than a full window to arrange a retirement, which is
      what the extension exists to prevent.

    **I am not neutral: I prefer what I enacted, and I would take (a) over leaving it unbounded if you
    want one sentence instead of four components.** What I would not do is leave the assurance standing,
    which is why §6.0-24 is corrected in this revision whichever way you answer.

    **STORE REVISION 36 — THIS ITEM IS REPAIRED AND PASS 11'S RECOMMENDATION HAS CHANGED. READ THIS
    BEFORE THE THREE OPTIONS ABOVE.** [MOO-106](/MOO/issues/MOO-106)'s pass 11, finding 1, establishes
    that **what I enacted did not bound the window** — the unbounded limb is now the **tolling in
    subsection M**, which store revision 33's own S-6 edit unbounded, and my bound reached the date only
    *"as extended under subdivision D 1."* All three options above were written against D 1's lateness,
    and **only option (a) reaches tolling.** So §6.0-24's assurance was false a second time.

    **What store revision 36 enacts**, and it is not a numeral choice:

    > <u>In no event shall a member's final qualifying retirement date, as extended under subdivision
    > D 1 or as tolled under subsection M, be later than 12 months after the date that applies under the
    > first sentence of this definition or, where a later date has been approved under this definition,
    > after that later date. Where the running of a period has been tolled under subsection M and the
    > member is prevented by the preceding sentence from retiring in a qualifying retirement after that
    > tolling ends, a retirement that satisfies every requirement of the definition of "qualifying
    > retirement" in this subsection other than the requirement that its effective date of retirement be
    > no later than the member's final qualifying retirement date, and that takes effect no later than the
    > first day of the second month following the month in which the Board acts upon the request the
    > filing of which tolled that period, is a qualifying retirement. Notwithstanding the two preceding sentences, a member's final qualifying
    > retirement date shall be no earlier than the first day of the second month following the month in
    > which the Board gives the statement required by this definition.</u>

    That is **pass 11's own repair for its option (a)** — M 2's protection preserved as a **deeming rule**
    rather than as an uncapped extension of the window — applied to the bound already in the text rather
    than to a new 21-month figure. It introduces **no figure the Code does not already have**, it leaves
    your 12 months, your three-month extension and your 120-day cap reading exactly as you decided them,
    and it closes finding 7 (the anchor had two readings three months apart) and finding 8 (the bound
    could close the window on a member the Board never told her dates) in the same three sentences.

    **What is still yours.** **Option (a)** — *"In no event shall a member's final qualifying retirement
    date be later than 21 months after the member's earliest qualifying retirement date"* — is still the
    tidiest single sentence, and pass 11 prefers it. Its costs are unchanged and are now better known: it
    introduces an unpriced numeral, and it needs the same deeming clause to avoid taking from the member a
    period another provision gives her. **Option (b)**, a 90-day cap on the lateness alone, no longer
    answers finding 1 at all and I withdraw it. **My recommendation is what is enacted.** If you take (a),
    one line goes to Fiscal & Impact Modeler at [MOO-116](/MOO/issues/MOO-116) to confirm that a 21-month
    absolute bound leaves cap utilisation unchanged, which should follow from the established result that
    reservations are certified on the anticipated **earliest** qualifying retirement date.

    **One thing not to do, and the reason is in your own record.** Pass 11's second cure was to deem a
    reconsideration request refused if the Board does not act within 120 days. That is the mirror image of
    the § 2.2-4021 B deemed decision that **§7 item 33 was withdrawn to avoid**, pointed at the member
    instead of at the Board. It is cheap, it works, and it takes a veteran's review right away because the
    Board was slow. I have not drafted it.
