40. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 33, from [MOO-95](/MOO/issues/MOO-95)'s pass 9, finding S-8. The over-cap
    reinstatement duty now carries four safeguards; the structural alternative is one sentence and I have
    not taken it.** G 4 requires the Board to reinstate a reservation released otherwise than in
    accordance with D 7 *"notwithstanding that the amount available to be reserved is thereby exceeded."*
    **Pass 9 accepts the choice** — the alternative is a member holding vested below-cost credit with no
    appropriation behind it, which is worse for the trust and worse under Va. Const. art. X, § 11 — and
    attacks what the sentence left out. **Its best point is one I had not seen: the sentence does not
    avoid the art. X, § 11 exposure, it relocates it.** Art. X, § 7 provides that *"[n]o money shall be
    paid out of the State treasury except in pursuance of appropriations made by law,"* so a settlement
    above the appropriation is not payable and the **trust carries the gap** — which is the single outcome
    the reservation architecture exists to prevent. And it does so silently: D 3's DPB confirmation is
    bypassed, because it is owed *"before reserving"* and reinstating is not reserving; D 3's
    money-committee notice is not triggered, because it attaches to a DPB refusal; and subsection K's
    shut-off is not triggered, because it attaches to a failure to appropriate. **The party who bears it
    is the wait-listed applicant, who appears in no stakeholder map and gets no notice saying why her
    turn moved.**

    **Enacted in store revision 33:** the contingency amount is reinstated with the reservation (pass 9's
    m-4 — *"it"* named only the reservation, and at $170,000 plus 20 percent the difference is $34,000);
    and the Board must give written notice of every reinstatement, of the amount of the excess and of the
    **cause of the release**, to the money-committee Chairmen **and to each applicant then on the waiting
    list**, within 15 days; must state every reinstatement and excess in the J report; and must accept no
    further election until the excess is eliminated. The last limb is already the law by operation of
    D 2; saying it stops the Board discovering it late.

    **The structural alternative, drafted and not inserted — and it is cheaper in every dimension except
    one.** *"A release of a reservation otherwise than in accordance with subdivision D 7 is void and of
    no effect, and the reservation shall be treated as never having been released."* On those words the
    headroom was never available, the cap is **never exceeded**, and the art. X, § 7 objection never
    arises. **The exception is the one that stopped me taking it**: the member accepted out of the
    wrongly-released headroom relied on a DPB confirmation and on the Board's acceptance, and void-ab-
    initio makes her acceptance ultra vires rather than valid-but-crowding. **That is a choice between two
    innocent members and it is yours, not mine.** If you take it, the four safeguards above stay useful
    and should stay. **Pass 9 also answered a sub-question I had asked it:** there is no time limit on
    reinstatement and that is correct rather than defective, because enactment 3 (ii) keeps the D 2
    limitations alive after 1 July 2033 expressly.

41. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 33, from [MOO-95](/MOO/issues/MOO-95)'s pass 9, finding M-10. A member can
    hold a reservation for up to two years, pay nothing, walk away at no cost, and file again — and the
    cure is a new rule, so it is yours.** Subsection H bars a further election by a member whose election
    *"has been settled under subdivision G 1 or G 4, or who has been credited with service credit under
    subdivision G 2, G 3, or G 5."* A member who pays **nothing** is credited with **zero** under each of
    those, which is not naturally *"credited with service credit"*; and revision 24's **D 6 to-zero
    branch is not in H's list at all**. Subsection F says in terms that *"no other consequence shall
    attach."* So the section contains no bar on, and no price for, parking a reservation.

    **The size, on pass 9's rerun of our own model.** A parker is accepted with a **$120,373** charge
    against the cap — the $100,311 mean reservation-basis certified subsidy plus the mandatory 20 percent
    contingency — takes a § 415 deferral to push the completion date out, and lets the reservation lapse
    up to **24 months** after the first completion date, which is the outer bound B 4 f's new cap sets.
    Central headroom is **$723,758, about six reservations**. **Six parkers close the programme**; on the
    published high take-up branch, where the cap is already 126 percent subscribed, one parker displaces
    a real applicant. Nothing in the costing models this, because it is behaviour the text permits rather
    than behaviour the text prices.

    Two cures, both drafted, neither inserted:

    - **(a) Price it — a minimum first payment**, in subsection E: *"<u>A reservation is released under
      subdivision D 7 if the member has not paid at least five percent of the amount determined under
      subdivision B 4 of § 51.1-142.2 within 60 days after the Board accepts the election.</u>"* **Pass 9
      prefers this and so do I**: it prices the behaviour rather than punishing it, it uses the five
      percent already in B 4 e so it adds no new figure, and it returns the headroom early rather than
      after two years.
    - **(b) Punish it — close H's gap**: *"<u>or whose election has lapsed under subdivision D 7 by
      reason of a failure to make any payment</u>."* Cheaper to draft, but it bars a member who may have
      had a perfectly good reason, and it does not return the headroom any sooner.

    **Scope note, because it bears on whether this is revision 24's fault:** D 7's lapse route is
    pre-existing. What revision 24 changed is that B 4 f's 24-month cap now fixes the holding period and
    the D 6 branch adds a costless full-refund exit, so the exposure is newly **sized**, not newly
    created.

42. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 33, from [MOO-95](/MOO/issues/MOO-95)'s pass 9, finding m-7. There is no
    anti-fraud provision, and the reason I have not drafted one is that it collides with a directive of
    yours.** § 51.1-124.11 reaches *"payment[s] to a member"* obtained by fraud; service credit obtained
    **below cost** by a false certification of military service, of creditable compensation or of an
    anticipated retirement date is not a payment to the member, and this section does not preserve or
    extend that remedy. The quantity at stake is up to **$170,000** of certified subsidy per member.

    **Why it is yours and not mine.** Any recovery provision is in direct tension with subsection F,
    which is the single most heavily-directed sentence in the bill: *"no amount shall be collected,
    offset, or withheld from any member, retiree, or beneficiary."* That came from MOO-15's due-process
    finding and MOO-35's **no clawback** direction, and I will not qualify it by drafting. The narrowest
    sentence that does the job without reopening the directive is a **fraud-only** carve-out, drafted and
    not inserted:

    > <u>Nothing in subsection F limits any remedy of the Board under § 51.1-124.11, or any other remedy
    > at law or in equity, in respect of service credit obtained under this section by means of a
    > statement or certification that the member knew to be false when made.</u>

    **I am genuinely neutral on this one.** It is defensible to say that a pilot of about nineteen
    members, every one of whose facts the Board certifies from its own records and from a DD-214, does
    not need a bespoke fraud remedy — and that is the honest reason it is not in the draft. It is also
    true that a committee member will ask, and that *"no amount shall be collected"* with no fraud
    exception is a sentence that reads badly when read aloud.




43. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 34, from [MOO-103](/MOO/issues/MOO-103)'s pass 10, finding S-1 — the
    serious finding of the pass, and the only place in the package where I have enacted a repair the
    reviewer routed to you. One phrase reverses it.** The bill's only quantity provision measured the
    months the member *needs* against the wrong defined term, and the words were **mine** — introduced
    by store revision 22 when I drafted option (c), not chosen by you.

    **The defect.** Clause (ii) of subsection C asks for *"the number of months of **creditable
    service** the member needs in order to have reached his unreduced retirement eligibility date"* and
    then measured that need against *"the **membership service** the member has earned."* § 51.1-124.3:
    *"'Creditable service' means prior service as set forth in § 51.1-142.2 **plus** membership service
    for which credit is allowable."* The unreduced date turns on **creditable** service, because
    § 51.1-153's unreduced conditions do. So the base was strictly narrower than the measure and the
    need was overstated by every month of non-membership credit the member holds — prior service
    purchased under subdivision B 1 or B 2, sick-leave credit under subsection D of § 51.1-142.2, and
    free military credit under subsection I. **A member aged 57 with 26 years of membership service
    plus 4 years purchased under B 1 (iv) needs 36 months and clause (ii) returned 84**, so the binding
    limb became clause (i)'s 48 months and she could buy **12 months of subsidised over-credit** with
    nothing to screen it: clauses (vi), (vii) and (viii) all ask whether the maximum purchasable amount
    gets her *to* the date, and 48 months got her past it.

    **What is in the text now:** <u>"taking account of all creditable service for which the member is
    entitled to credit as of that date"</u>. §6.0-26 item 2 records why that is four words longer than
    the reviewer's one-word fix — *"earned"* would read to exclude credit she bought or was granted,
    which is the whole of the affected population.

    - **(a) Keep the repair. Enacted, and my recommendation.** The agreed minimum-necessary rule is that
      the member buys the quantity her admission was assessed on, and admission under clause (v) is
      assessed on her unreduced date computed on **all** her creditable service. On the membership-service
      base, admission and quantity ran on different bases — which is the divergence **option (c) exists
      to remove**.
    - **(b) Restore the membership-service base.** One phrase, and you should say why in the record,
      because the next reader will read it as a slip:

      > In clause (ii) of subsection C, restore <u>"taking account of the membership service the member
      > has earned as of that date"</u> in place of <u>"taking account of all creditable service for
      > which the member is entitled to credit as of that date"</u>.

      **The only argument for it that I can construct** is that it keeps a member from counting credit
      she did not earn in service toward the quantity she may buy. I do not think it survives contact
      with clauses (v) to (viii), which all test her actual date, and it is paid for in over-credit.

    **The direction of effect, so nobody has to ask.** The repair **lowers** quantity and therefore
    cost, for members holding non-membership credit only. It cannot threaten the `$3,000,000`, the
    `$170,000` ceiling, the `[20]` percent contingency or the participant count, and **no published
    figure moves** — which is the problem in the next paragraph rather than a reassurance.

    **For Fiscal, and this is the live task.** S-1 is **unmeasured in every run to date**, because
    `build`/`mpa` run with `prior=0.0` throughout: no member in the cohort holds non-membership credit,
    so the engine cannot see either the defect or its repair. Clause (i)'s own words — *"four years,
    less any service credit previously purchased"* — show the population is not empty. What is needed
    is a prior-service-credit distribution on the cohort, and the two figures worth having are the share
    of live members for whom limb (ii) was the binding limb **before** the repair and is not after, and
    the aggregate over-credit the unrepaired text would have certified. **This is a model change, not a
    run**, and it is the second such request in this revision (see §0.x c for the first).

44. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 34, from [MOO-103](/MOO/issues/MOO-103)'s pass 10, finding Mi-10. One
    sentence, offered because it is free, and not enacted because nobody asked for it.** The month
    convention now in *"eligible member"* defines a month expressly. § 1-223 provides that *"'Month'
    means a calendar month"* and § 1-202 makes Title 1's definitions mandatory *"unless the construction
    would be inconsistent with the manifest intention of the General Assembly."* Defining a month in one
    clause therefore invites the negative implication that *"12 months"* **elsewhere** in the section —
    in subsection E, in *"final qualifying retirement date"*, and in subdivision B 4 f of § 51.1-142.2 —
    means something other than calendar months, with subdivision D 7 releasing the reservation if the
    member pays on day 363 of a 360-day year.

    Pass 10 rates this **minor** and largely self-answering, and I agree: the words *"for the purposes of
    this clause"* are themselves the evidence that § 1-223 was intended to govern everywhere else. If
    you want it closed rather than argued, one sentence at the end of the definition does it and costs
    nothing:

    > <u>Nothing in this definition affects the computation of any period under this section or under
    > § 51.1-142.2.</u>

    **I have not added it**, on the standing rule that I do not add a provision nobody agreed. Say the
    word and it goes in.

45. **Should the estate of a member who retired in a non-qualifying retirement and then died be rescued
    too?** Item 28's duty as approved applied on an approval *"or dies"*, which would have reversed G 4's
    second sentence — the [MOO-44](/MOO/issues/MOO-44) line — so **the death limb is enacted only for a case
    to which clause (i) applies**, the member who never retired at all. **I recommend leaving the reduced
    retiree where MOO-44 left her:** the gap item 28 cured was the Board acting before an approval could
    arrive, and a completed reduced retirement is not a race. Nine words would rescue her —
    [`moo64-record`](/MOO/issues/MOO-42#document-moo64-record) §7, enacted nowhere.
46. **The price of the bar you approved in item 28 — pass 10 raises it independently as a serious finding.
    While a disability application is pending the member may now be paid no retirement allowance at all.**
    The bar forbids the determination; **G 2's third sentence bars any allowance until that determination is
    made.** Pass 10's worked case is **14 months with no allowance**, and the arrears sentence makes her
    whole in money and not in time. One sentence in G 2 disapplies the bar during the pendency
    ([`moo64-record`](/MOO/issues/MOO-42#document-moo64-record) §7, enacted nowhere). **I recommend enacting
    it** — the no-recovery limb absorbs any overpayment, so the Commonwealth bears the cost either way and
    paying her is strictly better. **This is the one I would take first.**
47. **Does naming subsections A and C imply that a § 51.1-155.1 B or § 51.1-155.2 A retirement is outside
    the gateway?** [MOO-89](/MOO/issues/MOO-89)'s F-7 research, which returned after your answer on item 30,
    concludes the B member **passes** the positive limb — so **naming A and C without her is an expressio
    unius argument against her**, and against the § 51.1-155.2 A local official. **Nothing in the bill turns
    on it today**, neither being a *"state employee"*, so it is a hearing and reading risk, not a member's
    cheque. **I recommend enacting one sentence in each definition** rather than reopening B: *"The naming of
    a provision in this definition does not imply that a retirement under any other provision of this chapter
    is not a service retirement under § 51.1-153."*
48. **From pass 10's S4, and it is the only open item in this revision that can move a priced quantity. Does
    *"the earliest date on which the member may retire … under subsection A or C of § 51.1-155.1"* project the
    involuntary separation, or only the clock?** On the actual-facts reading — the basis on which I told you
    no figure moves — **the gateway in that definition does nothing at all**, every test using the term being
    applied while the member is in service. But § 51.1-155.1 A splits its conditions in time (20 years *"at
    the date of separation"*, entitlement *"upon attaining age 50"*), which invites the same projection the
    definition already performs on age and service. **On that reading the admission rule moves both ways:** a
    Plan 1 division director of 53 with 24 years is **excluded** under clause (v), and a member of 51 with 18
    years in the same position class is **admitted** who was not in the priced pool, her C (ii) cap falling
    from 48 months to 24. **Three ways
    ([`moo64-record`](/MOO/issues/MOO-42#document-moo64-record) §7): (a)** one sentence confining the route to
    a member who **has** incurred the separation — **my recommendation**, keeping your answer and confirming
    the priced basis; **(b)** pass 10's preference, **delete** the gateway from *"unreduced retirement
    eligibility date"* and keep it only in *"qualifying retirement"*, where the work is done — the reviewer
    traced the deletion through clauses (v) to (viii), C (ii), D 1, E and G 3 and found nothing breaks, and I
    agree with the trace; **(c)** leave it, **which I do not recommend** — the model ranks the admission rule
    as its second-largest driver.
49. **From pass 10's S5 and M15. The rescission duty item 28 created has no outer time limit, and by the time
    it operates the reporting duty may have closed.** § 51.1-156 H **requires** the Board to waive the 90-day
    application period for a disability the member could not have known of, so an approval can land **years**
    after a determination took effect — and by then the section may have expired and **subsection J's duty has
    closed**. **Store revision 33 already closed most of the over-cap half** (notice to the money committees,
    the excess and its cause, a J line, no further election). **What is left is time and tail: four fixes in
    [`moo64-record`](/MOO/issues/MOO-42#document-moo64-record) §7** — bound the trigger, add rescissions and
    restored credit to J 1, run J's tail to the last rescission, and name rescission and reinstatement in
    enactment 3. **I recommend all four; only the first has policy content**, because it takes from the member
    the § 51.1-156 H case. If you want only one, take the reporting three.
50. **From pass 10's M11 and m17. On a rescission, who bears the refund the Board has already paid, and may
    the member buy her whole credit back?** The no-recovery limb you approved was absolute; enacted inside
    G 4's existing prohibition it hangs on an **event**, and the Board could characterise recovering the
    G 2 (ii) refund as restoring the position before the determination rather than acting *"on account of"* the
    rescission. **The amount is small** — the sub-month remainder — **but the text does not decide who keeps
    it.** Candidate text ([`moo64-record`](/MOO/issues/MOO-42#document-moo64-record) §7) makes the refund
    irrecoverable and lets the member **or her employer** repay within 90 days to restore the whole, with no
    coercion. **The same item carries a stranding: subsection F's deeming sentence lists G 2, G 3 and G 5 and
    not G 4**; one word cures it. **I recommend both.**
51. **From pass 10's M10. The new D 1 notice duty has no consequence.** Nothing attaches if the Board gives a
    statement that omits the matters: an omission is not a *"determination"* reviewable under subsection M,
    is not a J item, and does not itself trigger the extension, which keys on the statement being **late** and
    not on its being incomplete. **One self-enforcing sentence:** *"Where the Board gives a statement or notice
    that does not state the matters required by this subdivision, the statement or notice is not given for the
    purposes of this subdivision until the Board gives the member those matters in writing."* **I recommend
    it, with the bite stated:** the periods that run from the giving of the statement would then run from the
    complete one.
52. **From pass 10's M12. Should the tolling item 28 required be capped, as subsection M 2's tolling is?**
    M 2 caps member-initiated tolling at 120 days; this tolling has **no cap**. Store revision 35 bounds the
    abuse as far as drafting can without a policy choice — *"pending"* is **defined** as from filing until the
    Board first acts, so reconsideration, a § 51.1-156 H waiver request and judicial review do not extend it.
    **A cap would close pass 10's free-option path** — a member who expects to fail subsection F filing an
    application she expects to lose, because death during pendency vests the whole election at the pilot price,
    up to **$170,000** — **and would re-create, for the member with a genuine 14-month application, the exact
    contradiction the tolling was enacted to cure.** **I recommend no cap** on the definition now enacted, and
    I would rather you saw the trade than had it chosen for you.
53. **A structural fact rather than a question about the bill, and part of it I have now had to act on.
    `bill-text-s8` is at the platform's 524,288-character ceiling, and two consecutive revisions have had to
    move a record out to fit.** Store revision 32 moved three §0 records, store revision 33 three more, and
    **store revision 35 moved store revision 33's and then replaced ten separate pointer stubs with the
    single table at §0.1** — because the stubs alone had reached about 14,700 characters and the alternative
    was being unable to enact your answers at all. **Nothing is lost**: every record and every stub is
    verbatim in
    [`bill-text-s8-revision-history`](/MOO/issues/MOO-42#document-bill-text-s8-revision-history). **What is
    left for you is the rest of the retained record** — §5's older citation checks and §6's older ambiguity
    notes, about 90,000 characters between them. **My recommendation: move them out in one pass and leave
    this file holding §1, the current revision's record, and the card.** It is a decision about the record
    rather than about the bill, and the next revision will face the same wall without it. **Store revision 37 has now had to take a third move**, and it took §8's status notes for revision 32 and older — **39,599 characters** — to the same document. This file arrived at store revision 37 with **8,473** characters of headroom and leaves it with **3,281**.
54. **From pass 10's M14. The member who paid in part is rescued on death and not on disability, and the
    difference is an accident of which subdivision the new limb names.** Item 28's clause lifts the settlement
    bar for a determination under **subdivision 2** only, so the member credited pro rata under **G 3** who is
    then approved for disability gets **no rescission and no settlement**, while the full payer who merely
    missed her window gets everything restored — and **G 4's own death branch expressly rescues the partial
    payer.** **The cure is two words — *"a determination under subdivision 2 or 3"*, with restoration on the
    death branch's formula.** **I recommend it for parity with the death branch**, and I flag that it is a
    money decision: it settles a subsidy for a member who never completed payment, at the pro-rata credit, a
    case no figure in §3 prices separately.




55. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 36, from [MOO-106](/MOO/issues/MOO-106)'s pass 11, findings 11 and 12, and it
    is a privacy choice rather than a drafting one.** G 4 requires a **state agency** to notify the Board
    of the involuntary separation of *"any member whose election the Board has accepted"* within 15 days.
    Store revision 36 gives that duty a consequence — every failure of which the Board becomes aware is
    reportable under subsection J — and re-keys the Board's own 15-day member notice so it no longer runs
    from the date the Board *"learns"* of a separation nobody told it about. **What neither edit can fix is
    that the agency may have no way to know the duty applies to it.** Nothing tells an agency that the
    Board accepted an election; B 4 e gives a **paying** employer knowledge, and G 4 expressly contemplates
    transfer — *"A transfer of the member's employment from one state agency to another state agency shall
    not affect an election"* — so the separating agency may never have been the agency that knew. A member
    who elects at Agency A, transfers to Agency B and is separated by Agency B loses the branch through
    nobody's fault.

    **Two cures, each one sentence, and they trade a member's privacy against her protection.**

    - **(a) Tell the employer.** *"<u>The Board shall notify the member's employing state agency that the
      Board has accepted the member's election, and shall notify any state agency to which the member's
      employment is transferred of which the Board receives notice, no later than 15 days after the
      acceptance or after receiving notice of the transfer.</u>"* It makes the duty performable and
      **discloses to the member's employer that she has bought military credit in order to retire early.**
      In a cohort of about nineteen that is not a small disclosure, and the employer is the party whose
      separation decision the branch exists to protect her from.
    - **(b) Put it on the member.** Key the agency's duty to a member who has given the agency written
      notice of her election, and add a corresponding statement to the D 1 acknowledgment. **It keeps the
      choice with the member and loses the protection for every member who does not make it** — which,
      predictably, will include the member who is about to be separated and does not know it.

    **I am not neutral but the choice is not mine: I would take (b)**, because the bill's whole design
    keeps the election between the member and the Board and (a) reverses that for one notice. **Pass 11
    routes the separating agency to Stakeholder Analyst as a party bearing a real cost who is not mapped**,
    and that is independent of which cure you take.

56. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 36. Pass 11 attacked the three candidate texts you have not inserted, and
    found a serious defect in each. None of this is in the bill; all of it changes what you would be
    adopting.** The full attack is at
    [`pass11-disposition`](/MOO/issues/MOO-42#document-pass11-disposition) §3.

    - **Item 41(a) — the minimum first payment — must not be adopted as drafted, and the reason is item
      40's reason.** As written it *"releases the reservation and leaves the election accepted."* D 5 says
      no member acquires the right to the pilot price *"before the Board has reserved the certified
      subsidy"* — she already has — so on day 61 she holds an accepted election at the pilot price with
      **no reservation behind it**, may pay in full in month 11, and subsection E obliges the Board to
      credit, G 1 to settle and the Commonwealth to pay **against nothing**. That is the Va. Const. art.
      X, § 7 exposure item 40 is about, created by the cure for item 41. Three repairs, all drafted in the
      item: make it cancel the election as well as release the reservation and put it in **D 7**, which is
      the closed list of release triggers, rather than in E pointing at D 7; say whether the five percent
      must be **member money**, because B 4 e already requires a different five percent to be *"from funds
      other than funds paid by an employer"*; and run the 60 days from the later of acceptance and the
      date the employer's filed B 4 e policy first applies to her, because a member relying on her
      employer's next-fiscal-year policy is doing exactly what B 4 e contemplates and will be 60 days in
      with nothing paid. **With those three repairs I still prefer (a) to (b), and so does pass 11.**
    - **Item 42 — the fraud carve-out — rests on a premise that is half wrong, and pass 11's conclusion is
      cleaner than the item's.** It re-opened **§ 51.1-124.11** at source: the section reaches *"[a]ny
      payment to a member or beneficiary which is later determined by the Board to have been procured on
      the basis of any false statement,"* recoverable *"by way of a credit against future payments."* Item
      42 is right that **service credit obtained below cost is not a payment** — and wrong that the gap is
      therefore open, because **once the member retires the inflated allowance payments are recoverable
      already**. On that framing the honest question is whether a bespoke provision is needed at all, and
      **pass 11 leans to no. So do I, and I am withdrawing item 42's implicit recommendation.** If you take
      it anyway, three repairs are drafted: cut *"or any other remedy at law or in equity,"* which lifts
      subsection F for setoff against the allowance and is precisely MOO-15's due-process finding and
      MOO-35's no-clawback direction; exclude a survivor, beneficiary or estate that did not participate in
      the statement; and confine it to statements of **existing fact** rather than to *"an anticipated
      retirement date,"* which is an intention. And note what it does **not** reach: G 1's *"No obligation
      of the member arises after the member's effective date of retirement"* and G 4's *"nothing in this
      subdivision creates any liability of the member"* both survive it untouched and would both be
      pleaded.
    - **Item 33 — the § 51.1-124.11 substitute — carries the same defect as the sentence it replaced, and
      I say so about my own substitute.** *"Reasonable prior written notice"* in § 51.1-124.11 is prior to
      a **fraud-recovery** determination — one inherently accusatory, pre-decisional act. Adapted to *"a
      determination under this section"* it imposes a **pre-determination** notice-and-hearing duty on
      every determination in § 51.1-142.4, including D 4's eligibility determination, which the Board must
      make within 30 days, and D 2's $170,000 refusal, which runs on a 30-day clock. **The whole notice
      architecture of this section is post-determination** (*"no later than 30 days after making it"*), so
      the substitute contradicts it nine times over. And *"an opportunity to be heard in accordance with
      the Administrative Process Act"* is an Article 3 proceeding, which is what **§ 2.2-4021 B's deemed
      decision attaches to** — so the substitute either re-imports the default judgment I withdrew the
      first candidate to avoid, or is empty. **Pass 11 ranks that PLAUSIBLE rather than confirmed; I treat
      it as decisive enough not to recommend the substitute.** Its repair, which I do recommend if you want
      review in the bill, is drafted in the item: notice **of** a determination to each person adversely
      affected, an opportunity to be heard **on reconsideration** rather than before the determination, and
      the General Assembly's own Article 5 formula for judicial review. Your third option — delete M 2's
      last sentence and say nothing — remains honest and remains the cheapest.

57. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 36, from pass 11's finding 6, and it is the residual of a deletion rather than
    a defect.** **B 4 g is deleted** — see §0.x a and §6.0-36 item 6 — because a § 415 limitation year is a
    **per-plan** attribute that *"may be changed only by amending the plan"*, so a statutory designation is
    either surplusage or a change the regulation does not permit, and in neither case can it be confined to
    a subset of a plan's participants. **If you want a designation kept anyway**, the compliant version is
    one sentence and it is confined to the subdivision rather than to the plan:

    > <u>For the purposes of this subdivision, the limitation year is the calendar year.</u>

    **ANNOTATED IN STORE REVISION 39, and the candidate above should not now be adopted.**
    [MOO-117](/MOO/issues/MOO-117)'s pass 12, finding **F-8**, opened 26 C.F.R. § 1.415(j)-1 at source and
    established what the record had not drawn: (a) makes the calendar year the limitation year **"unless
    the terms of a plan provide otherwise"** — a **default**, not a rule — and (d) provides that once
    established it **"may be changed only by amending the plan."** A sentence saying *"for the purposes of
    this subdivision, the limitation year is the calendar year"* asserts the same plan-document fact the
    deleted B 4 g asserted, one level down, and is wrong for the same reason. **Store revision 39 goes the
    other way and defines the term by reference to the plan**, which is correct whatever the plan says.
    **If you still want a designation, the only compliant route is a plan amendment by the Board and not a
    sentence in this bill.**

    It says in the Code what B 4 a already says in the same subdivision, which is why I did not enact it.
    **The question underneath it is a fact about VRS's plan document that the bill cannot determine** —
    whether a limitation year is designated, and whether Plan 1, Plan 2 and the § 51.1-169 hybrid defined
    benefit component are one plan or more for § 415 purposes — and it is with Statutory Researcher at
    [MOO-115](/MOO/issues/MOO-115). **Pass 11 asks that it reach the memo's risk section either way**, and
    I agree: the exposure was a qualification risk reaching members the pilot excludes, including teachers
    and local employees, in a bill whose subsection L tells localities it imposes no duty on them.

58. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 36. Pass 11 names five things for the memo's risk section and one of them is
    now a presentation decision rather than a drafting one.** Four of the five are answered in the text by
    this revision — the window's bound, the separated member's 90 days, the B 4 g designation and the
    over-cap reinstatement's missing appropriation duty. **The fifth is not mine and I have not touched
    it:** the **interest-free deferral residual**, which the record already sizes at **$11,000 to $13,000
    per deferring member**, borne by the **trust** and **outside the certified subsidy**, because B 4 f's
    24-month cap fixes for the first time the outer size of a deferral at a price struck on the original
    valuation date. Pass 11 confirms independently that it belongs in the § 30-19.1:7 statement as a
    quantified, one-sided omission. **The decision is whether it is disclosed there as a feature or whether
    the deferral is priced** — and pricing it means charging statutory interest on a deferral the member
    did not ask for and § 415 compelled, which I would not draft without being told to.

59. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 37, carried over from [MOO-92](/MOO/issues/MOO-92) and re-cut on four
    findings of [MOO-104](/MOO/issues/MOO-104). The partial-lump-sum tier notice — drafted in full,
    recommended, and deliberately not in the bill.** A member admitted to this pilot stands at the
    unreduced-retirement door **by construction**: clauses (vi) and (viii) of *"eligible member"* admit
    only a member who reaches her unreduced retirement eligibility date within a month of applying, and
    § 51.1-165.01 B measures its partial-lump-sum tiers in **years of creditable service for service
    performed after** that door. **For the § 51.1-153 B 2 cohort the bill models** — age 50 with 30
    years, which is the route [MOO-52](/MOO/issues/MOO-52) and this document's own editorial note use —
    the 1x tier is reachable only at the very end of the retirement window and the 2x and 3x tiers are
    not reachable within it at all. **The bill does not warn her.**

    **One premise of this item was wrong and is corrected, because it is a citation failure.** I wrote
    that the 2x and 3x tiers are *"not reachable at all by a member who retires in a qualifying
    retirement."* § 51.1-165.01 **B 1** clause (i) is a **disjunction**: *"has satisfied the requirements
    for normal retirement after January 1, 2004, **or** has been credited with at least three years of
    creditable service for service performed after the date he was first eligible for an unreduced
    service retirement allowance."* **The first disjunct requires no post-eligibility service and
    unlocks all three tiers**, and nothing in clauses (i) through (viii) of *"eligible member"* confines
    participants to the B 2 route — so a member who reaches her unreduced date by the § 51.1-153 **A**
    normal-retirement route is eligible for C 1, C 2 and C 3 on the day she retires. The conclusion holds
    for the modelled cohort and for no one else. S10-F4; routed to Statutory Researcher.

    **The text, three pieces and all three or none.** Into the definition of *"final qualifying
    retirement date"*, after the sentence that requires the Board's written statement:

    > <u>The Board shall state in that written statement (i) that the election of an option under
    > § 51.1-165, or of a partial lump-sum option under § 51.1-165.01, does not prevent the member's
    > retirement from being a qualifying retirement and does not change the cost at which the service
    > credit purchased under the member's election vests under subdivision G 1, and (ii) the number of
    > years of creditable service for service performed after the member's unreduced retirement
    > eligibility date with which the member would be credited if the member retired on the member's
    > final qualifying retirement date, and which of the partial lump-sum options described in
    > subdivision C 1, C 2, or C 3 of § 51.1-165.01 that number would make available to the member under
    > subsection B of that section.</u>

    > <u>Where the Board does not state in that written statement every matter this definition or
    > subdivision D 1 requires it to state, the member's final qualifying retirement date is extended by
    > the number of days from the date the statement was required to be given to the date on which the
    > Board states the omitted matter to the member in writing, and the Board's failure to state any such
    > matter is a determination of the Board for the purposes of subsection M.</u>

    And, in the last sentence of the same definition, *"as extended under subdivision D 1"* becomes
    <u>"as extended under subdivision D 1 or under this definition"</u>.

    **Four things the review changed about this text, and each matters.** **(1) Limb (ii) is now a
    computation about this member, not a construction of the Code.** It used to say *"the effect of
    subsection B of § 51.1-165.01 upon the member"*, which the Board discharges at lowest cost by
    reciting the subsection — a notice that is *fully compliant* and leaves the member not knowing the
    one fact the item exists to tell her (S10-F7). The new form is determinate, computable from facts the
    Board already holds, binds nobody outside the pilot, and **survives the citation failure above**,
    because the Board applies B 1's disjunct to the member in front of it instead of to a false general
    premise. **(2) The consequence is now direct.** The earlier draft made an incomplete statement *"not
    given"* so that subdivision D 1's existing day-for-day extension would carry it. D 1 bites on a
    statement the Board **gives**, so an **uncured** omission produced no extension at all — and the
    member could not compel the cure, because an omission is not a *"determination"* within subsection M
    2 (S10-F5). The new sentence extends the date itself **and** makes the failure M-reviewable, so the
    duty has an enforcer. **And this is the same question as §7 item 51**, which
    [MOO-103](/MOO/issues/MOO-103)'s pass 10 raised independently against subdivision D 1's new notice
    duty and which recommends exactly the *"not given"* sentence S10-F5 tested and rejected. **Items 51
    and 55 should be answered together, and the form above is the one I recommend for both** — it
    extends the date itself rather than suspending the giving of the statement, it names the failure as a
    determination so the member has a route to subsection M, and store revision 33's outer bound catches
    it once the eleven words below are added. **(3) It reaches subdivision D 1's own requirement.** The single most
    consequential item in that statement — *"[t]he Board shall state the period allowed by this
    subdivision"*, the member's 30 days to file her § 51.1-153 notice of retirement — is required by
    **D 1** and not by the definition, so *"this definition or subdivision D 1"* is not tidiness
    (S10-F6). **(4) The extension is capped, and the cap already exists.** Store revision 33's outer
    bound catches an extension *"under subdivision D 1"* only; eleven added words in that sentence bring
    this one inside it, so the new consequence adds **no numeral** and cannot reopen the window. That is
    my own catch and it is the reason the third piece is not optional.

    **No figure moves and nothing is repriced.** The two routes that would make the tiers actually
    reachable — lengthening the window, or disapplying § 51.1-165.01 B's clock for pilot members — are
    policy and I recommend neither; the second would add a **third amended section** to an act that
    amends one and adds one. **And you should know what limb (i) does, because it is accurate and it
    cuts against the pilot.** The 1x lump sum is twelve times the monthly allowance — of the order of
    **$35,700** on a 30-year, $70,000-AFC member — and for the modelled cohort it is reachable only by
    staying to the end of the window. **Limb (i) is therefore a Board-certified written assurance whose
    practical message is "stay the full twelve months,"** against a Program whose purpose is earlier
    vacancies and against the very thing **subdivision J 3** instructs the Department of Planning and
    Budget to measure. Fiscally that is the **cheap** direction and the model's own `WINDOW_BOUND` note
    already prices the last-permissible-day case; the exposure is programmatic (S10-F8). **If you want
    the notice without that message, say so and limb (i) comes out — but then the member is not told
    that electing a PLOP is costless, which is the one thing about it she can rely on.**

60. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 37, and it is the only fatal finding anyone has returned on this bill since
    pass 4. [MOO-104](/MOO/issues/MOO-104)'s S10-F1: in the death case the purchased credit can produce
    no benefit at all while the Commonwealth settles and pays for it. Both premises of the finding need
    correcting and the choice is still yours.** The chain is on the face of the text. **Subdivision D 1
    compels** the member to file her § 51.1-153 notice of retirement within **30 days** of the Board's
    written statement. **§ 51.1-165 C 1** then provides that *"in **all cases** where the death of the
    member occurs prior to the effective date of retirement but after the Board has received written
    notification of the member's effective date of retirement, benefits shall be paid in accordance with
    the provisions of § 51.1-163"* — and § 51.1-163 pays only *"any excess of his accumulated
    contributions … over the total retirement allowances received by him."* Its only exception,
    § 51.1-165 C 2, requires a **joint and last-survivor option** naming a **spouse, minor child or
    parent**. So the compelled filing is what brings C 1 down on her.

    **Correction 1 — the survivor half is narrower than the finding states, and for most members no
    drafting helps.** A member who had **actually retired** on a single-life allowance and then died
    would be paid under § 51.1-163 as well. For the unmarried participant with no minor child and no
    surviving parent, *"the credit is worth nothing"* is what Chapter 1 does for anyone in her position.
    **That is not a defect this bill created.** The class the bill genuinely aggravates is one the review
    did not isolate: **a member who elected an option under § 51.1-165 naming a contingent annuitant who
    is not a spouse, a minor child or a parent** — a sibling, an adult child, an unmarried partner. Had
    she lived to her effective date, her annuitant would have taken the survivor annuity her option
    bought, increased by the purchased credit. She dies three weeks short, C 2 does not reach her
    annuitant, C 1 routes to a refund, and **the option is defeated by a filing the bill required her to
    make.**

    **Correction 2 — the fiscal half is real, and it does not run through the deeming.** The review
    located the overstatement in the deeming's effect on clause (i) of *"certified subsidy."* **No
    deeming is enacted, and the exposure survives, because the definition has a floor:** *"[t]he
    certified subsidy shall not be less than the amount by which the standard cost of the service credit
    to be purchased, determined as of the valuation date stated in the certification, exceeds the amounts
    payable by the member and by any employer under subdivision B 4 of § 51.1-142.2."* Subdivision G 4
    redetermines *"in the manner provided in subdivision 1"*, that manner is the definition, and the
    floor is pinned to the **original** valuation date, so it does not fall when the benefits do.
    **In a death case the benefit comparison is therefore irrelevant: the amount settled is the floor,
    capped at the amount reserved.** [MOO-105](/MOO/issues/MOO-105) has priced the identical shape for
    the subsection C workers'-compensation death — **$106,253** of certified subsidy per event, central,
    p90 **$152,558**, plus **$29,153** of the member's own money, for *"a credit worth $0."*
    [MOO-104](/MOO/issues/MOO-104) §5 puts the ordinary filed-notice death at **0.053 expected events**
    across the pilot on the Lab's own fitted mortality. **So this is immaterial in expectation and
    material per event — an equity and precedent question, not a fiscal one** — with a named loser the
    stakeholder map does not have: the **employing state agency** that adopted a subdivision B 4 e policy
    and paid 95% of the price, which G 4 refunds only *"where the member dies before completing
    payment."*

    **Three answers, each drafted, and none of them free.**

    - **(a) Preserve the elected option — the narrow bridge, for the class in Correction 1.** Into
      subdivision G 4, after the death sentence:

      > <u>Notwithstanding subsection C of § 51.1-165, where the member had elected an option under
      > § 51.1-165 or § 51.1-165.01 before the date of death, the benefit payable on the member's death
      > shall be determined as though the member had retired on that date in the retirement and under
      > the option the member elected.</u>

      **What it costs you.** It **names a section in order to displace it**, which is a deliberate
      partial reversal of MOO-96's F-4 and F-6 — the position that the chapter's own routing should
      govern unnamed. The reviewer accepts that an option (a) *"must therefore carry a notwithstanding"*;
      I am telling you that the *notwithstanding* **is** the reversal and not a technicality, and that
      naming § 51.1-165 C in one place raises exactly the negative implication against § 51.1-163 that
      F-4 was about. It also increases the trust's liability in the cases it reaches, which no document
      has priced.
    - **(b) Cap what the Commonwealth settles — and it must override the floor or it does nothing.**
      In subdivision G 4's redetermination sentence, for *"the amount settled shall be the amount so
      redetermined or the amount last certified and reserved for the election, whichever is less"* read:

      > <u>the amount settled shall be the least of the amount so redetermined, the amount last certified
      > and reserved for the election, and, where the settlement is by reason of the member's death, the
      > increase attributable to the service credit credited under this subdivision in the actuarial
      > present value of the benefits payable under this chapter on the member's death, determined by the
      > Board using the actuarial assumptions adopted for the most recent actuarial valuation of the
      > retirement system; and the sentence of the definition of "certified subsidy" in subsection A that
      > fixes a minimum does not apply to a redetermination under this subdivision</u>

      **What it costs you.** The floor exists to keep the **trust** whole: the trust granted service
      credit and is entitled to its standard cost. Disapplying the floor does not remove the loss, it
      **moves it from the appropriation to the trust fund** — which is the same place MOO-96's F-3 put
      the uplift, and it engages the same two instruments, **Va. Const. art. X, § 11** and the **Va. Code
      § 30-19.1:7** impact statement. (b) is smaller than (a) and does not reopen F-4. It is not cheaper
      in substance; it is cheaper to the General Fund.
    - **(c) The one I would take first, because it is one clause and it names the party who actually
      loses.** Extend G 4's employer refund to the case the credit produced nothing:

      > <u>Where the settlement of a certified subsidy by reason of the member's death produces no
      > increase in the actuarial present value of the benefits payable under this chapter on the
      > member's death, the Board shall refund to any employer that paid on the member's behalf under
      > subdivision B 4 e of § 51.1-142.2 the amount the employer paid, no later than 60 days after the
      > settlement.</u>

      **What it costs you.** It is an addition, so it is yours; it is the only one of the three that
      compensates a real identified loser rather than re-routing a trust-fund entry; and it leaves the
      Commonwealth's payment alone, so it does **not** answer the "paying for nothing" objection — it
      answers the "and the agency lost $10,830" one. **(a), (b) and (c) are independent and any
      combination is available.**

    **What I will not do.** I will not pick one. The reviewer's own words are that this is *"a policy
    choice, not a drafting choice"*, and three of these four paragraphs are about **who bears a loss**.
    **And whichever you choose, one thing does not go away:** with § 51.1-162, § 51.1-163 and
    § 51.1-165 cited nowhere in the bill — which is the right trade and [MOO-104](/MOO/issues/MOO-104)
    §7 reaffirms it — the collision is **invisible on the face of the act**. A drafter's note is not
    enacted and will not be read by a committee. **This has to travel in two other places: the memo's
    risk section, and the drafting request to the Division of Legislative Services.** It is not
    sufficient that the Lab knows.

    **NEW ON PASS 13 — [MOO-118](/MOO/issues/MOO-118) makes this worse in a specific way, and adds two
    answers.** Its three serious findings are **one question**, and it is narrower and harder than the
    one above: not *how little* the survivor is paid, but **what the benefit is measured on** — which the
    text does not say. **F1:** every member in the pilot is, by clauses (v) and (viii) of *"eligible
    member"*, a member who **cannot** lawfully take an unreduced service retirement until the credit is
    credited, and *"qualifying retirement"* is defined as one whose allowance **is not reduced** under
    § 51.1-155 A 2 or A 3. So the Board is ordered to pay *"as though the member had retired in a
    qualifying retirement"* on a date when she could not have taken one. **Store revision 37's clause made
    that textually settled rather than arguable**, because *"no other requirement"* closes the residual
    set and the unreduced element sits outside the criterion — a consequence of the middle treatment, not
    a reversal of it. **F2:** re-anchoring *"notwithstanding"* to the crediting left the **payment** duty
    alive in the partial-payment case with no stated measure, so the Board either pays on months the
    member never funded while the estate and the employer get that money back in cash, or it has no
    computable amount at all. **F3:** the measure G 4 specifies differs from § 51.1-162 B's on all four
    mechanics — actual date against deemed age 55 or 60, unreduced against the joint-and-survivor
    allowance, and expressly *"as though no option had been elected"* — and there is **no priority rule**.
    **The frequency bound is 0.1013 expected deaths in the window**, so this is severe in law and about a
    one-in-ten event in fact.

    **(d) Name the measure.** One sentence at the end of the death branch: <u>"The benefit payable under
    this subdivision shall be the benefit the chapter provides on the death of a member in service,
    computed on the service credit credited under this subdivision."</u> **It is the only answer that
    closes all three serious findings at once**, and it needs **no section named**, so it does not reopen
    MOO-96's F-4. **It also makes the *"as though"* hypothetical, the three date disapplications and store
    revision 40's own enumeration surplusage** — so its cleanest form **replaces** them rather than adding
    to them, and taking (d) means deleting about 700 characters I have just written, which is the right
    trade and I am naming it so it is not discovered later. **What it costs you:** it forecloses the
    survivor's argument that the General Assembly promised her an **unreduced** allowance, which is what
    the present words appear to say. The reviewer's view is that the Board will pay the § 51.1-162 B
    measure anyway, as the only computable one, so (d) codifies the outcome rather than changing it — but
    it is still money and it is still your choice, not mine.

    **(e) The partial-payment measure, and it goes to you whole.** Two clauses inside G 4's fourth
    sentence: <u>"the benefit payable under the preceding sentence shall be computed on the service credit
    credited under this sentence, and the member shall not be treated as having reached his unreduced
    retirement eligibility date by reason of the election"</u> — the second being subdivision **G 3's own
    guard**, which G 4's partial-payer branch does not have. **I did not enact either half alone**, and
    that is deliberate: naming the service base alone, or importing the guard alone, settles the
    partial-payment death on the reading that makes the premise unconstructible in **every** such case,
    which is precisely the defect [MOO-104](/MOO/issues/MOO-104) rated **fatal** in the clause it
    withdrew. **(d) subsumes (e)'s first half**; take (d) and (e) reduces to the guard, which is then
    harmless.

    **ANSWERED AND PARTLY ENACTED IN STORE REVISION 45 — and the answer makes one of the unselected
    options more urgent, not less.** The card was accepted on 12 October 2026 with **(d)** and **F8's
    notice duty**. Both are in the text: the measure sentence is enacted verbatim as approved, the
    deeming and all four disapplications are deleted (972 characters), and the notice is lifted out of
    the partial-payment branch to cover every death. **(a), (b) and (c) are not selected and stay live
    here. (e) is closed: (d) subsumes its first half, and its second half is unnecessary, because the last
    sentence of subsection E already withholds the unreduced retirement eligibility date from every member
    who has not paid in full. That corrects what this item said about (e) and what MOO-118's F2 rests
    on.**

    **Why (a) is now the live question.** (d) says the benefit is *"the benefit this chapter provides on
    the death of a member in service."* For every member who has filed the notice of retirement
    **subdivision D 1 compels** her to file, the chapter's answer is **§ 51.1-165 C 1 → § 51.1-163**, a
    refund of her own contributions, unless C 2's spouse, minor child or parent saves her. **(d) names
    the measure; it does not displace the routing.** The elected-option class — Correction 1 above — is
    therefore unanswered in words that are now **settled rather than arguable**, which is a change in
    kind: before this revision a survivor could at least argue that the deeming had made her member a
    retiree on the date of death and that her option took effect. **That argument is gone.** (a) is the
    only drafted answer that reaches her, it is 236 characters, and its cost is the one stated above — it
    names § 51.1-165 C in order to displace it, a partial reversal of MOO-96's F-4.

    **Two smaller things this revision leaves for you, both named rather than drafted in.** G 4 sets **no
    date by which the Board must credit** in the death case, so the new notice duty is bounded in content
    and not in time (§6.0-45 item 5) — a crediting deadline is a new duty and is yours. And the notice's
    review limb makes subdivision **M 2's class list** load-bearing in a second place: it reaches *"a
    member, retiree, or beneficiary"* and so reaches neither the estate nor a personal representative,
    which is item 61's third answer.

    ---

    **ANSWERED 12 OCTOBER 2026 — SIX OF NINE. ENACTED IN STORE REVISION 54. (a), (b) AND (c) REMAIN
    DRAFTED AND UNENACTED AND THIS ITEM STAYS OPEN FOR THEM.**

    **Selected and enacted:** **(f)** keep a death in service a death in service; **the pre-filing option
    notice**; **the three disclosure and reporting repairs** as a set; **subsection M standing for the
    estate**, form 1; **the crediting deadline** at 60 days after the Board learns of the death; and
    **release of the settled subsidy** where the survivor takes the contributions instead. Seven edits,
    all inside new § 51.1-142.4 — subdivisions G 4, D 1 and M 2.

    **Not selected and still live:** **(a)** preserve the elected option — the only drafted answer that
    reaches a member who named a contingent annuitant who is not a spouse, minor child or parent;
    **(b)** cap the settlement, which does nothing unless it expressly disapplies the *"certified
    subsidy"* floor; **(c)** refund the employer where the credit produced nothing. Each is word for
    word at [`moo126-disposition`](/MOO/issues/MOO-97#document-moo126-disposition) §7 and unchanged.

    **What (f) did to the fatal finding.** [MOO-126](/MOO/issues/MOO-126)'s **F1** is answered for the
    class the bill actually costs — a member survived by a spouse, minor child or parent who elected no
    option. **It is not answered for the elected-option class**, which is (a), and (a) was not selected.
    That class is now in a worse position than before store revision 45 in one respect and a better one
    in another: § 51.1-163 no longer takes the whole benefit, but § 51.1-162 B reaches only the three
    relations, so a sibling, an adult child or an unmarried partner named as contingent annuitant still
    receives nothing from the purchased credit. **That is a decision this card made and not an oversight.**

    **Two corrections this item carries forward, both already made.** (d) is **gone** — superseded by (f),
    with its computation limb carried into (f) word for word; anything above that reasons from (d) as
    live text is reasoning from a sentence that is no longer in the bill. And the paragraph above that
    says M 2 *"reaches 'a member, retiree, or beneficiary'"* was wrong twice over: the live list was four
    classes, and as of store revision 54 it is **five**, including the personal representative of a
    deceased member's estate, in both of M 2's lists.



61. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 37. Three answers to items already on your list, so that none of them needs
    a fresh decision from you except where it always did.**

    **1 — Subdivision M 2's 120-day aggregate tolling cap: confirmed by counsel, and I am no longer
    asking you to move it.** [MOO-92](/MOO/issues/MOO-92) found M 2's tolling uncapped and repeatable;
    store revision 24 had already cured it independently as pass 7's F-1 — second and subsequent requests
    toll nothing, and *"the aggregate of all tolling under this subsection shall not exceed 120 days."*
    **Both limbs are my numerals**, and MOO-92's own candidate had been 90 days, so I put the figure to
    you. [MOO-104](/MOO/issues/MOO-104) §6 answers it: 120 days *"is not unsafe on its own numbers"* — a
    third of a 12-month window for a process the Board must complete in 90 days — and **excluding repeat
    requests is the stronger of the two choices** because it removes the incentive to serialise rather
    than merely capping it. *"I would not reopen 90 vs 120."* **No action needed. The figure stands at
    120 and it is now supported by a document rather than by me.**

    **2 — Item 39 gains a third alternative, from a second reviewer who reached it independently.**
    Store revision 33 bounded subdivision D 1's lateness extension at 12 months, which closed the
    uncapped half of the problem. What [MOO-104](/MOO/issues/MOO-104)'s S10-F6 says is that the half that
    matters is the **absolute** bound — *"the only provision that would make the window a bounded
    quantity, and the window is the fiscal model's dominant assumption"* — and that it is the one thing
    in that finding he *"would insist on."* His text, as a third option alongside item 39's (a) and (b):

    > <u>In no event shall the member's final qualifying retirement date be later than 18 months after
    > the member's earliest qualifying retirement date.</u>

    **18, against pass 9's 21 and against the 12 + 3 + 120 days + 12 months the text now delivers.**
    Same objection as to item 39's (a), and it is a real one: an absolute bound can **cut into** the
    three-month processing extension and the 120 days of tolling you have already decided, taking from a
    member a period another provision gives her. **The new information is not the numeral; it is that two
    independent reviewers have now said the absolute form is the one to take.** Item 39 is where you
    answer it.

    **3 — Item 34 item 2 should carry two classes, not one.** That item has been waiting since store
    revision 30 for an extension of commission to widen subdivision M 2's *"a member, retiree, or
    beneficiary"* to include an **applicant**, because subdivision D 4's refusal notice promises a review
    to the one class M 2 excludes. [MOO-104](/MOO/issues/MOO-104)'s S10-F10 finds a second class missing
    from the same sentence: the **estate**, which subdivision G 2 already pays and which subdivision G 4
    already requires the Board to notify *"of the reasons for the determination"* — a duty that means
    nothing if there is no one who may ask for reconsideration. Combined:

    > In subdivision M 2, for *"A member, retiree, or beneficiary"* read <u>"A member, retiree,
    > beneficiary, applicant, or personal representative of a deceased member"</u>, and make the same
    > change to *"a member, retiree, or beneficiary aggrieved"* in the last sentence of that subdivision.

    **One amendment to one sentence, not two.** Using two words for one concept, or amending the same
    sentence in two successive revisions, is how a cross-reference gets stranded. **And one residual I am
    flagging rather than drafting around:** where the refund is of the order of $4,400, § 64.2-601 lets a
    successor collect without administration, so there may be **no personal representative** to exercise
    the right. If you want that covered the word is *"successor"* as § 64.2-601 uses it, and it is an
    addition on top of an addition.

62. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 39, from [MOO-117](/MOO/issues/MOO-117)'s pass 12, finding F-12, which pass 12
    routed to you in terms — *"this is the Director's call, not mine."* The over-cap reinstatement now
    requests an appropriation, and nothing happens if the answer is no.** Store revision 36 gave the
    reinstatement a duty to *"request a confirmation under subdivision D 3 in respect of the amount of any
    resulting excess."* D 3's confirmation is framed as a **precondition to reserving**, and here the
    reservation has already been reinstated by command *"notwithstanding that the amount available to be
    reserved is thereby exceeded."* D 3's machinery for a **negative** answer runs entirely on an
    **application**: waitlist it, notify the applicant, re-request annually *"until the application is
    accepted."* So on a refusal the Board is commanded to waitlist an application that does not exist and
    notify an applicant who is a retiree. **Nothing requires the excess to be cured, funded or reduced.**
    Two routes, and I recommend the first:

    - **(a) Say what a refusal does, and drop the inoperative request.** The consequence is already in the
      text — the excess goes in the subsection J report and the Board accepts no further election until it
      is eliminated, which store revision 39 has now defined. One sentence replaces the D 3 request:

      > <u>Where the Director of the Department of Planning and Budget or his designee determines that
      > funds appropriated and legally available are not sufficient to fund a reinstatement under this
      > subdivision, the Board shall state that determination and the amount of the excess in the report
      > required by subsection J and shall accept no further election until the excess is eliminated, and
      > subdivision D 3 does not otherwise apply to the reinstatement.</u>

    - **(b) §7 item 40's structural route** — make an unlawful release **void ab initio**, so the
      reservation never left and no excess ever arises. It never exceeds the cap and the art. X, § 7
      objection never arises, **and it makes the acceptance of the member who relied on a DPB confirmation
      ultra vires.** That is still a choice between two innocent members and it is still yours.

    **What pass 12 adds to item 40 that I had not seen:** the exposure is not avoided by the reinstatement
    sentence, it is **relocated**. Art. X, § 7 means a settlement above the appropriation is not payable,
    so the **trust** carries the gap — the single outcome the reservation architecture exists to prevent.
    **And N-17 matters here as much as F-12:** pass 12 opened both constitutional sections at source and
    found **no** art. X defect in edits 9, 10 and 11. The fiscal risk is real; the constitutional version
    of it, which is the version the opposition will plead, is wrong.

63. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 39, from pass 12's F-13. Does the new excess certification engage subsection
    K?** The reinstatement duty has the Board *"certify the amount of the excess to the Governor and to
    those Chairmen as an amount necessary to fund the purposes of this section."* Subsection K's trigger is
    an amount certified *"as necessary to fund the certified subsidies the Board expects to reserve in that
    fiscal year and to administer this section."* **The words do not match**, so on the better reading
    edit 9's certification engages nothing and is a letter. If a reader holds that it does engage K, the
    consequence is that the Board *"shall accept no election filed on or after the first day of that
    fiscal year"* — **a whole-year freeze**, heavier than the reinstatement sentence's own *"no further
    election until the excess is eliminated"* and inconsistent with it. Four words settle it either way:

    > <u>…as an amount necessary to fund the purposes of this section, which certification is not a
    > certification for the purposes of subsection K.</u>

    **I recommend that form** — the stop-and-resume rule the same sentence already contains is the
    proportionate consequence, and a whole-year freeze triggered by one reinstatement would close the
    programme for reasons unconnected to the appropriation. The opposite form is the same sentence with
    *"not"* deleted. **It moves no figure either way**, but the two forms are not the same programme, so I
    will not pick by drafting.

64. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 39, from pass 12's F-14. The reinstatement is characterised as a determination
    for subsection M, and that promises a remedy to the wrong person.** The reinstatement is
    **commanded**, so reconsideration cannot change it and neither can APA review of an agency that did
    what the statute ordered. The member whose reservation is reinstated is the one person M 2 plainly
    covers and she **benefits**. The **wait-listed applicant** is the one adversely affected — the Board
    *"shall accept no further election until the excess is eliminated"* — and the same sentence forbids her
    the record she would need, because her notice *"shall not state the cause of the release and shall not
    identify any member."* Store revision 39 has given her the fallback recitals the other six adverse
    notices carry (F-20), which is notice content and not a remedy. Three options, all drafted:

    - **(a) Drop the subsection M characterisation and say so expressly**, so the over-promise goes:
      > <u>A reinstatement under this subdivision is not a determination of the Board for the purposes of
      > subsection M and requires no act of any applicant.</u>
    - **(b) Give her the remedy she actually needs — priority:**
      > <u>Where the Board accepts no further election by reason of an excess arising under this
      > subdivision, the Board shall accept the applications then on the waiting list maintained under
      > subdivision D 5, in the order determined under subdivision D 4, before accepting any application
      > filed after the date of the reinstatement.</u>
    - **(c) Leave it.** The characterisation costs her a letter and the Board a reply.

    **I would take (b) with (a)**, because (b) is the only one of the three that changes anything for the
    person bearing the cost. **(b) is a priority rule and therefore yours** — it reorders who gets a
    scarce $3,000,000 and no cited document directs it.

65. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 39, from pass 12's F-21. Does the employer that pays up to 95 percent of a
    member's cost get a right of review, or do we say expressly that it does not?** The employer appears
    in the section eight times as a payer and a refundee and **never once as a holder of a right.** M 2
    runs to *"a member, retiree, or beneficiary adversely affected"*, and an employer is none of the three.
    Store revision 36's widening of the giving rule to *"a member or an applicant"* — now *"a person"* on
    F-11 — made the omission look deliberate rather than accidental. The failure case is concrete: an
    agency pays $38,000 of a member's cost, the member does not retire, and G 2 refunds the agency *"in
    proportion to the amounts each paid"* — but if the Board miscomputes the proportion, or applies the
    agency's money to buy the member additional service credit under the adverse-tax-consequence route
    that subsection F now expressly blesses, **the agency has no way to contest it.** Two one-line forms:

    - > <u>…a member, retiree, beneficiary, **or employer** adversely affected by a determination…</u>
    - > <u>An employer that pays any amount under subdivision B 4 e of § 51.1-142.2 on a member's behalf
      > has no right of review under this subsection.</u>

    **I am genuinely neutral** and the reason to answer it is that silence is now the worst of the three:
    it leaves a litigable question about standing in a bill whose notice architecture is otherwise
    complete. Pass 12 also routes the paying employer to Stakeholder Analyst as a **third** unmapped party
    bearing a real cost, after the wait-listed applicant and the separating agency.

66. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 39, from pass 12's F-10. How wide should enactment 3 clause (i) be?** It
    preserves *"every right of **a member** … and every period allowed to **a member**."* Store revision
    36's edit 4 widened the giving rule in the same revision from *"the member"* to *"a member or an
    applicant"*, which is strong evidence that *"a member"* does **not** include an applicant — and makes
    the narrow reading of clause (i) the better one. The casualty is real: an applicant wait-listed under
    D 3 who gets her pre-expiry notice on 1 June 2033 and files an M 2 reconsideration of the waiting-list
    placement on 25 June 2033 has a 90-day right that **dies with the section on 1 July 2033**, because
    clause (i) preserves the Board's duties only *"for every election **accepted** before that date"* and
    hers was never accepted. The same gap reaches the beneficiary's and the estate's periods. One phrase:

    > <u>…every right of a member, **applicant, retiree, beneficiary, survivor, or estate** … and every
    > period allowed to **any of them**…</u>

    **I recommend it and I have not enacted it**, because it extends the Board's duties past the sunset to
    a class nobody has costed, and because pass 12 routed it to you rather than to me. **It moves no money
    figure**, but it is a sunset decision and the sunset is the bill's most load-bearing political feature.

67. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 39, from pass 12's F-17. The agency-notice report line is self-blinding, and
    the cure is a new duty.** The Board must state *"every failure of a state agency to give that notice
    **of which the Board becomes aware**"* — and it becomes aware only when it learns of the separation
    from somewhere else. **The failures that actually harm members are exactly the ones the metric cannot
    see**, so an agency that never notifies produces a report showing zero failures and the General
    Assembly reads a clean number. Pass 12's cure uses data that already exists, because J 2 already
    obliges DHRM and the Department of Accounts to report separation data to Planning and Budget:

    > <u>The Board shall reconcile the elections it has accepted against the separation data reported
    > under subdivision J 2, no less often than quarterly, and shall state every discrepancy in the report
    > required by subsection J.</u>

    **I recommend it and I have not enacted it**, because it is a new continuing administrative duty with a
    cost, and no document in the authorities directs it. **Note one thing before you answer:** store
    revision 39's F-6 and F-7 cures reduce the harm this metric misses, because the extension no longer
    moves silently and a late-discovered separation now rescinds the determination. The reporting gap
    remains; the member's loss largely does not.

68. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 39, and it is mine rather than pass 12's. The floor is the one component of
    the retirement window with no figure bounding it, and I have stated that rather than cured it.**
    Store revision 39 makes the floor a **date-setter** (F-4), which removes the twelve-month ambiguity
    that was the finding. What it does not do is bound it: the floor is *"the first day of the second month
    following the month in which the Board gives the statement required by this definition"*, so where the
    Board gives the statement very late the member's final qualifying retirement date follows the Board's
    own act, **and the reservation stays charged against the $3,000,000 until it does.** Two candidate
    bounds, neither enacted:

    - **(a) Cap the lateness the floor can absorb:**
      > <u>…the member's final qualifying retirement date is that first day, except that where the Board
      > gives that statement more than 90 days after the time required by this definition the member's
      > final qualifying retirement date is the first day of the second month following the expiration of
      > those 90 days.</u>
      Bounded, and it **cuts off a member for the Board's default**, which is the trade.
    - **(b) Route the very-late statement to G 4 clause (iii)**, as store revision 39 does for the
      statement that is never given, by replacing *"at a time when the Board has not given the statement"*
      with *"at a time when the Board has not given the statement within 90 days after the time required by
      this definition."* The member keeps her credit at the pilot price and no window is extended — but she
      must retire to get it, and a subsidy is paid where the acceleration may be smaller than certified.

    **I would take (b) if either**, and I am content to leave it stated. **Three reasons it is not urgent:**
    it arises only on the Board's own total or near-total default; D 1 already requires every such delay to
    be stated in the subsection J report, so it is visible; and the member is never worse off than she
    would be under a bound. **The reason it is on your list at all** is that I have twice told you this
    definition's components were each bounded on the face of the text and have twice had to withdraw it.
    This is the remaining one, named before anybody finds it.

69. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 39, from pass 12's F-26, which pass 12 expressly left out of scope and so do
    I. The estate's money is applied to buy service credit for a decedent.** G 4's death-branch refund goes
    *"to the member's estate and to any employer"*, and the adverse-tax-consequence route — which store
    revision 36's edit 7 has now expressly blessed in subsection F for D 6 as well — directs the Board to
    *"apply that amount to the purchase of additional service credit **for the member**."* **The member is
    dead.** Buying service credit for a decedent is incoherent, and on the face of the text the estate's
    money disappears into it. It is pre-existing and it is not a store revision 36 edit; pass 12 recorded
    it because edit 7 touched the sentence. The narrow cure:

    > <u>Nothing in this section requires or permits the Board to apply any amount to the purchase of
    > service credit for a member after the member's death, and any such amount shall be paid to the person
    > entitled to any benefit payable on the member's death or, if there is none, to the member's
    > estate.</u>

    **I recommend it and I have not enacted it for one reason:** the adverse-tax-consequence route exists
    because a refund may not always be payable without a federal tax consequence, and I cannot tell you
    whether paying it to an estate re-creates that consequence. **That is a tax question and not a drafting
    one.** If the answer is that it does not, the sentence above is the fix; if it does, the right answer is
    a different one and it is not mine. Worth one line to Statutory Researcher before you decide.

70. **NEW IN STORE REVISION 41, from [MOO-119](/MOO/issues/MOO-119)'s pass 13, findings F-3 and F-5 —
    the one serious finding this revision does not enact, and the reviewer's own repair for it does not
    reach two of the three routes.** An **accepted election under which no service credit is purchased**
    has no defined operation anywhere downstream, and since store revision 38 no provision prohibits one.
    Subdivision D 4 is mandatory — *"The Board shall accept elections in the order in which eligible
    members file complete applications"* — and the Board cannot supply a refusal power by procedure,
